# Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise

Mingchen Ma

University of Wisconsin-Madison

mingchen@cs.wisc.eduSupported by NSF Award CCF-2144298 (CAREER).Guyang Cao

University of Wisconsin-Madison

gcao8@wisc.eduSupported by NSF CAREER Award CCF-2440563.Jelena Diakonikolas

University of Wisconsin-Madison

Jelena@cs.wisc.eduSupported in part by the Air Force Office of Scientific Research under award number FA9550-24-1-0076, NSF CAREER Award CCF-2440563, and NSF MFAI Award DMS-2502282.Ilias Diakonikolas

University of Wisconsin-Madison

ilias@cs.wisc.eduSupported by NSF Medium Award CCF-2107079,
ONR Award number N00014-25-1-2268, and an H.I. Romnes Faculty Fellowship.

###### Abstract

We study the problem of learning a drifting concept in the
presence of Massart noise. In this framework,
an online learner has access to a history
of independent samples whose labels are noisy versions
of a target concept that may change from round to round.
The goal is to output, in each round, a hypothesis with
small prediction error.
We study the complexity of this learning
problem for the fundamental class of margin-separable
linear classifiers (halfspaces).
On the positive side, we give a
computationally efficient learner achieving error
η+O~​(Δ1/3/γ)\eta+\tilde{O}(\Delta^{1/3}/\gamma),
where η\eta upper bounds the Massart noise rate,
Δ\Delta is the drift rate, and γ\gamma is the margin.
Interestingly, in the realizable setting,
an adaptation of our techniques
yields an efficient learner with an improved error
rate over prior work.
On the lower-bound side, we provide formal evidence of an
information-computation tradeoff, strongly suggesting that
our algorithm’s performance is essentially optimal.
Specifically,
while the information-theoretically optimal error
scales with Δ1/2\Delta^{1/2}, we prove
that Δ1/3\Delta^{1/3}-scaling is unavoidable for
low-degree polynomial tests,
even in the special case of random classification noise.

## 1 Introduction

Statistical learning theory \[ [Vap98](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx41 "")\] typically
focuses on settings in which the training set consists of i.i.d. samples
from a fixed unknown distribution, and the performance
of the trained model is evaluated on the same distribution.
This modeling assumption does not capture
a range of machine learning applications,
where the training data is drawn from a non-stationary distribution
that drifts over time, and the deployed model needs to perform well
given data drawn from a different distribution.
Examples include financial prediction, analysis of consumer preferences,
weather forecast, and more recently language models.
Specifically, in language model training \[ [ADMR23](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx2 "")\],
the models are trained over static snapshots of text
collected from websites that are frequently changing.
When the models are deployed, they need to work with timely news,
facts, or programming APIs.
To address such settings, we require learning models and associated
algorithms that succeed in the presence of distribution drift.

A prototypical statistical model to formalize such non-static
settings is that of binary classification with a drift \[ [HLO91](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx28 ""), [HL94](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx27 ""), [Bar92](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx5 "")\].
For concreteness, we formally define this model below.

###### Definition 1.1(Learning under Distribution Drift).

Let 𝒞\mathcal{C} be a hypothesis class of {±1}\{\pm 1\}-valued functions over
some domain XX. For t=1,2,…,t=1,2,\dots, let D(t)D^{(t)}
be a distribution over X×{±1}X\times\{\pm 1\} such that
dT​V​(D(t),D(t+1))≤Δd\_{\mathrm{T}V}(D^{(t)},D^{(t+1)})\leq\Delta, where dT​Vd\_{\mathrm{T}V} is the total variation distance
and Δ∈(0,1)\Delta\in(0,1) is the drift rate.
The error of a hypothesis h:X→{±1}h:X\to\{\pm 1\}
with respect to D(t)D^{(t)} is
err(t)​(h):=𝐏𝐫(x(t),y(t))∼D(t)\[h​(x(t))≠y(t)\]\mathrm{err}^{(t)}(h):=\operatorname\*{\mathbf{Pr}}\_{(x^{(t)},y^{(t)})\sim D^{(t)}}\[h(x^{(t)})\neq y^{(t)}\].
A learning algorithm 𝒜\mathcal{A} is given
a sequence of examples {(x(t),y(t))}\{(x^{(t)},y^{(t)})\},
for t=1,2,…t=1,2,\dots, where (x(t),y(t))∼D(t)(x^{(t)},y^{(t)})\sim D^{(t)},
and works in an online fashion. Specifically,
given {(x(i),y(i))}i=1t−1\{(x^{(i)},y^{(i)})\}\_{i=1}^{t-1}, 𝒜\mathcal{A} outputs a hypothesis
h^(t):X→{±1}\hat{h}^{(t)}:X\to\{\pm 1\} and
then receives an example (x(t),y(t))(x^{(t)},y^{(t)}) drawn from D(t)D^{(t)}.
The goal of the learner is to minimize err(t)​(h^(t))\mathrm{err}^{(t)}(\hat{h}^{(t)}),
as compared to optt:=minh∈𝒞⁡err(t)​(h)\mathrm{opt}\_{t}:=\min\_{h\in\mathcal{C}}\mathrm{err}^{(t)}(h).

This model has been extensively studied over the past decades
(see Section [1.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#S1.SS2 "1.2 Related Work ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise")).
Much of the early work focused on determining the optimal error rate for general hypothesis classes.
Specifically, for any class 𝒞\mathcal{C} of VC-dimension
dd, \[ [HLO91](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx28 ""), [Bar92](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx5 ""), [HL94](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx27 ""), [BL97](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx7 ""), [Lon99](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx34 "")\] establish
the optimal error rate of Θ​((d​Δ)1/2)\Theta((d\Delta)^{1/2}) for the realizable case
(i.e., when the labels in each round are consistent with a function in 𝒞\mathcal{C})
and the excess error rate of Θ​((d​Δ)1/3)\Theta((d\Delta)^{1/3})
in the agnostic setting.
A key idea underlying these works is the following:
When dT​V​(D(t),D(t+1))d\_{\mathrm{T}V}(D^{(t)},D^{(t+1)}) is small,
by carefully selecting a number of past samples that
balance the estimation error and the error due to the distribution drift,
empirical risk minimization (ERM) over the selected sample gives a
hypothesis with statistically optimal error.

Unfortunately, the aforementioned statistically optimal
approach leads to an exponential time algorithm
in general—even in the realizable setting,
where each x(t)x^{(t)} is labeled
according to a hypothesis h(t)∈𝒞h^{(t)}\in\mathcal{C}.
More broadly, despite extensive investigation,
the computational complexity of learning with distribution drift
remains poorly understood, even for basic hypothesis classes.
Of course, if the non-drift version of the problem (corresponding to Δ=0\Delta=0)
is computationally intractable, the hardness is inherited in the drift setting.
Hence, it is natural to focus on concept classes and models of label noise
for which algorithmic results are known in the non-drift setting.

Here we study the complexity of drift learning
for the class of linear classifiers or halfspaces.
A halfspace is any function of the form hw​(x)=sign​(w⋅x)h\_{w}(x)=\mathrm{sign}(w\cdot x), where
ww is a weight vector and sign​(t)\mathrm{sign}(t) is equal to 11 for t≥0t\geq 0 and −1-1 otherwise.
Let ℋ:={hw​(x)=sign​(w⋅x)∣w∈𝕊d−1}\mathcal{H}:=\{h\_{w}(x)=\mathrm{sign}(w\cdot x)\mid w\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\} denote the class of halfspaces
on ℝd\mathbb{R}^{d}, where 𝕊d−1\mathbb{S}^{d-1} is the unit ball.

Prior algorithmic results for drifting halfspaces
are restricted to the realizable setting and
either achieve highly suboptimal error rates \[ [HL94](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx27 "")\]
or require very strong distributional assumptions \[ [CMEDV10](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx12 ""), [HKY15](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx26 "")\].
Departing from this setup,
we aim to develop _efficient_ 111Henceforth, “efficient” refers to polynomial-time algorithms in parameters describing the problem (d,1/γ,1/Δd,1/\gamma,1/\Delta).  algorithms
under minimal distributional assumptions
and in the presence of label noise.
More specifically, our only assumption on the distribution of examples
will be the existence of margin γ>0\gamma>0 with respect to the target halfspace.
Regarding the noise model, we assume that the labels in each
round are corrupted by Massart noise \[ [MN06](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx36 "")\],
one of the classical semi-random noise models in the literature for
which efficient learners
are known in the non-drift setting \[ [DGT19](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx15 "")\]222In the agnostic model,
known hardness results rule out
efficient algorithms even with a margin assumption \[ [Dan16](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx13 "")\]..

In the Massart model, the label of example xx can be flipped
with probability η​(x)≤η<1/2\eta(x)\leq\eta<1/2, independently across
examples. The noise function η​(x)\eta(x) is assumed to be bounded and
is _unknown_ to the learner. The special case of Massart noise
where η​(x)=η\eta(x)=\eta for all xx
is known as Random Classification Noise (RCN) \[ [AL88](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx3 "")\].

In the drift setting, we allow a different source of Massart
noise in each round. Specifically, let hw(t)​(x)h^{(t)}\_{w}(x) be the target function (halfspace)
and let ηt​(x):𝕊d−1→\[0,η\]\eta\_{t}(x):\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\to\[0,\eta\]. For t≥1t\geq 1, we say that
a distribution D(t)D^{(t)} is realized by (hw(t),ηt​(x))(h^{(t)}\_{w},\eta\_{t}(x))
if for every xx,
ηt​(x)=𝐏𝐫(x,y)∼D(t)\[hw(t)​(x)≠y∣x\].\eta\_{t}(x)=\operatorname\*{\mathbf{Pr}}\_{(x,y)\sim D^{(t)}}\[h^{(t)}\_{w}(x)\neq y\mid x\].
Under [Definition˜1.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#S1.Thmdefinition1 "Definition 1.1 (Learning under Distribution Drift). ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise"), we will restrict every D(t)D^{(t)}
to be realized by some unknown (hw(t),ηt​(x))(h^{(t)}\_{w},\eta\_{t}(x)). (The case of RCN
corresponds to ηt​(x)≡ηt≤η\eta\_{t}(x)\equiv\eta\_{t}\leq\eta.)

### 1.1 Our Results

Our work provides the first results for drifting concept classes under Massart noise, in terms of both statistical and computational complexity.
For statistical complexity, in [Section˜2](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#S2 "2 Information-Theoretic Baselines under Massart Noise ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise"), we show that for a general hypothesis class with VC dimension dd, the statistically optimal excess error with Massart noise—achieved by an ERM—is Θ~​(d​Δ)\tilde{\Theta}(\sqrt{d\Delta})
(and Θ~​(Δ/γ)\tilde{\Theta}(\sqrt{\Delta}/\gamma) for γ\gamma-margin halfspaces)—as opposed to Θ​((d​Δ)1/3)\Theta((d\Delta)^{1/3}), which applies to adversarial label noise.333The
“tilde” notation hides logarithmic factors in the argument.

We then characterize the error that can be achieved by _efficient_ learning algorithms for learning γ\gamma-margin drifting halfspaces with Massart noise.
Our main algorithmic result is the following:

###### Theorem 1.1(Main Algorithmic Result).

Consider the problem of learning drifting γ\gamma-margin halfspaces
with η\eta-Massart noise. There is an algorithm 𝒜\mathcal{A} that runs in poly​(d,1/γ,1/Δ)\mathrm{poly}(d,1/\gamma,1/\Delta) time and outputs a hypothesis h^(T)\hat{h}^{(T)} such that
for any time step T=Ω~​(Δ−2/3)T=\tilde{\Omega}(\Delta^{-2/3}), with
probability at least 9/109/10,
err(T)​(h^(T))≤η+O~​(Δ1/3/γ)\mathrm{err}^{(T)}(\hat{h}^{(T)})\leq\eta+\tilde{O}(\Delta^{1/3}/\gamma).

To the best of our knowledge, this is the first non-trivial error guarantee
achieved by an efficient learning algorithm in the presence of label noise.
We note that, for the special case of RCN, an adaptation of our algorithm
gives an error of optt+O~​(Δ1/3/γ)\mathrm{opt}\_{t}+\tilde{O}(\Delta^{1/3}/\gamma).
As another important implication, we obtain an efficient algorithm with an even smaller error O~​(Δ​γ−3/2)\tilde{O}(\sqrt{\Delta}\gamma^{-3/2})
for the realizable setting—improving
upon the O~​(Δ​γ−2)\tilde{O}(\sqrt{\Delta}\gamma^{-2}) error achieved
by the algorithm in \[ [HL94](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx27 "")\].

Interestingly, the error achieved by our noise-tolerant drift algorithm
scales with Δ1/3\Delta^{1/3},
which is qualitatively worse than the statistically optimal error
(that scales with Δ1/2\Delta^{1/2}).
We complement our upper bound by providing formal evidence that
the scaling with Δ1/3\Delta^{1/3} is unavoidable for efficient learning algorithms,
even in the special case of RCN. Specifically,
we establish a lower bound against the class of
low-degree polynomial tests \[ [Hop18](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx29 ""), [KWB19](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx33 ""), [Wei25](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx42 "")\]—one of the most well-studied computational models
in the context of statistical-computational tradeoffs that captures a broad class of learning algorithms.
Specifically, we show:

###### Theorem 1.2(Informal Implication of [Theorem˜4.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1\#S4.Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 4.1. ‣ Hardness Construction ‣ 4 Low-Degree Polynomial Hardness ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise")).

Consider the problem of learning drifting γ\gamma-margin halfspaces with (1/3)(1/3)-RCN. For T≥γ−1/6​Δ−2/3T\geq\gamma^{-1/6}\Delta^{-2/3}, there is a family of instances such that
under the low-degree hardness conjecture, there is no polynomial time algorithm that achieves error optT+Δ1/3​γ−1/6\mathrm{opt}\_{T}+\Delta^{1/3}\gamma^{-1/6} error for every instance in the family.

An intuitive interpretation of Theorem [1.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#S1.Thmtheorem2 "Theorem 1.2 (Informal Implication of Theorem˜4.1). ‣ 1.1 Our Results ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise") is that efficient algorithms
for our problem require excess error Ω​(Δ1/3)\Omega(\Delta^{1/3}), matching the guarantee
of our algorithm. Taken together, these results illustrate the following
phenomenon for bounded label noise (Massart, RCN) settings
in terms of scaling with drift Δ\Delta: while information-theoretic rates place
these label noise models in the same category as the realizable setting—scaling
with Δ1/2\Delta^{1/2}—computationally efficient algorithms need to pay the higher
Δ1/3\Delta^{1/3} price—the information-theoretic limit of the agnostic setting.

### 1.2 Related Work

##### Learning with Distribution Drift

As already discussed, the early line of work on learning drifting concepts focused on establishing optimal excess error bounds
for a general hypothesis class with VC-dimension dd,
under the classical assumption that the TV-distance between consecutive distributions is upper bounded by Δ\Delta\[ [HLO91](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx28 ""), [Bar92](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx5 ""), [HL94](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx27 ""), [BL97](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx7 ""), [Lon99](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx34 "")\].
In recent years, variants of this problem have been studied \[ [HY19](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx30 ""), [HHW24](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx25 ""), [MU23](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx37 ""), [BTN+25](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx9 "")\] to develop methods that do not depend on the prior knowledge of Δ\Delta.
Importantly, all algorithms in these works
rely on ERM
and are thus not computationally efficient in general.

The aforementioned progress notwithstanding,
the computational complexity of learning with drift is poorly understood.
Early work \[ [HL94](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx27 "")\]
gives an LP-based efficient algorithm for realizable halfspaces
on ℝd\mathbb{R}^{d} with error O~​(d​Δ)\tilde{O}(d\sqrt{\Delta})
(a bound that is suboptimal by a d\sqrt{d} factor).
More recent works focused on developing efficient learners
under different error metrics \[ [MMM12](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx35 "")\]
or under strong assumptions on the marginal distribution—namely that each
Dx(t)D^{(t)}\_{x} is the a uniform distribution over the unit sphere.
Specifically, \[ [CMEDV10](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx12 "")\] gave an efficient algorithm
with error O​((d​Δ)1/4)O((d\Delta)^{1/4}) that was later improved to O​(d​Δ)O(\sqrt{d\Delta})
by \[ [HKY15](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx26 "")\]. To the best of our knowledge, no efficient algorithms under the distribution drift settings were known to tolerate label noise.

##### Halfspace Learning with Label Noise

Designing efficient learners for halfspaces in the presence of label noise
has been a central problem in learning theory that has seen substantial progress in the past decades. In the non-drift version of the problem, when the marginal distribution is sufficiently structured (e.g., uniform over the unit sphere or Gaussian),
efficient agnostic learners that can tolerate adversarial label noise are known \[ [ABL17](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx1 ""), [DKS18](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx19 ""), [DKTZ22](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx20 ""), [DKM24](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx17 "")\]. For more general distributions (e.g., in the presence of margin),
known hardness results \[ [Dan16](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx13 "")\] rule out polynomial-time agnostic learners.
In the presence of Massart noise, recent work \[ [DGT19](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx15 ""), [CKMY20](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx10 ""), [DZ24](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx24 ""), [CKST24](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx11 ""), [KMT24](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx32 "")\] has developed efficient learners achieving error η+ϵ\eta+\epsilon,
a guarantee shown to be optimal for efficient algorithms \[ [DK22](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx16 ""), [NT22](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx38 ""), [DKMR22](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx18 "")\].

##### Low-Degree Polynomial Hardness

Proving lower bounds against restricted algorithmic families such as SQ algorithms \[ [Kea98](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx31 "")\] or low-degree polynomial estimators \[ [Hop18](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx29 ""), [BBH+21](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx6 "")\]
is a popular method of studying the computational complexity of learning problems.
Low-degree polynomial estimators (defined in [Section˜4](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#S4 "4 Low-Degree Polynomial Hardness ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise"))
provide a natural computational model for our online learning setting.
For recent progress on low-degree polynomial hardness, we refer the reader to \[ [Wei25](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx42 "")\].

### 1.3 Notation

For a halfspace hw​(x)=sign​(w⋅x)h\_{w}(x)=\mathrm{sign}(w\cdot x) and x∈𝕊d−1x\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}, we define err(i)​(w,x):=𝐏𝐫(x,y)∼D(i)\[hw​(x)≠y∣x\]\mathrm{err}^{(i)}(w,x):=\operatorname\*{\mathbf{Pr}}\_{(x,y)\sim D^{(i)}}\[h\_{w}(x)\neq y\mid x\].
For γ∈(0,1)\gamma\in(0,1), an example x∈𝕊d−1x\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1} is said to have γ\gamma-margin with respect to a halfspace hw​(x)h\_{w}(x) if \|w⋅x\|≥γ\left\|w\cdot x\right\|\geq\gamma. A distribution DD is said to have γ\gamma-margin with respect to hw​(x)h\_{w}(x) if 𝐏𝐫x∼Dx\[\|w⋅x\|<γ\]=0\operatorname\*{\mathbf{Pr}}\_{x\sim D\_{x}}\[\left\|w\cdot x\right\|<\gamma\]=0.
For a,b∈ℝa,b\in\mathbb{R}, we denote by ϕ​(a,b)=sign​(a)​sign​(b)∈{±1}\phi(a,b)=\mathrm{sign}(a)\mathrm{sign}(b)\in\{\pm 1\}. We use Oδ​(⋅)O\_{\delta}(\cdot) notation to hide a log⁡(1/δ)\log(1/\delta) factor and use O~​(⋅)\tilde{O}(\cdot) to hide the polylogarithmic factors in the argument inside the big-Oh.

## 2 Information-Theoretic Baselines under Massart Noise

The literature on learning drifting concepts with label noise
predominantly focuses on the agnostic setting (adversarial label noise), where for every hypothesis class with VC dimension dd, the
optimal excess error is Θ​((d​Δ)1/3)\Theta\left((d\Delta)^{1/3}\right)\[ [Lon99](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx34 "")\].
However, agnostic learning is known to be computationally hard for natural hypothesis and
distribution classes, even without distribution shift
(i.e., for Δ=0\Delta=0). Since our focus in this work
is on computationally efficient algorithms, we consider structured
label noise, for which we establish different excess error baselines.

We first show that in the presence of Massart noise, a smaller excess error rate of O~​(d​Δ)\tilde{O}(\sqrt{d\Delta}) can be obtained (see [Section˜B.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#A2.SS1 "B.1 Proof of Theorem˜2.1 ‣ Appendix B Omitted Proofs from Section˜2 ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise") for the proof). This result, summarized in the following theorem, establishes an _information theoretic_ upper bound; however,
the algorithm itself is not polynomial-time.
We highlight that the stated upper bound
applies to any Boolean concept class
(not just halfspaces) and the general case of Massart noise.

###### Theorem 2.1(Information-Theoretic Upper Bound).

Consider the problem of learning drifting binary concepts. Let ℋ:X→{±1}\mathcal{H}:X\to\{\pm 1\} be a hypothesis class with VC dimension dd. Suppose that for every i>0i>0, D(i)D^{(i)} is realized by ((h∗)(i),ηi​(x))((h^{\*})^{(i)},\eta\_{i}(x)) for some (h∗)(i)∈ℋ(h^{\*})^{(i)}\in\mathcal{H} and ηi\eta\_{i} that satisfies the η\eta-Massart noise condition. There is an algorithm 𝒜\mathcal{A} such that for every t=Ω~(d/((1−2η)Δ)t=\tilde{\Omega}(d/((1-2\eta)\Delta), 𝒜\mathcal{A} outputs a hypothesis h^(t)\hat{h}^{(t)} such that with probability at least 9/109/10 the error of h^(t)\hat{h}^{(t)} is at most optt+O~​((d​Δ/(1−2​η))1/2)\mathrm{opt}\_{t}+\tilde{O}((d\Delta/(1-2\eta))^{1/2}).

On the lower bound side, we specialize to halfspaces with η\eta-RCN (the
more specialized the class, the stronger the lower bound).
We give a matching Ω​((d​Δ)1/2)\Omega((d\Delta)^{1/2})
bound on the excess
error, as summarized in [Theorem˜2.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#S2.Thmtheorem2 "Theorem 2.2 (Information-Theoretic Lower Bound). ‣ 2 Information-Theoretic Baselines under Massart Noise ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise")
(see [Section˜B.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#A2.SS2 "B.2 Proof of Theorem˜2.2 ‣ Appendix B Omitted Proofs from Section˜2 ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise") for the proof).
Combined with [Theorem˜2.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#S2.Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 2.1 (Information-Theoretic Upper Bound). ‣ 2 Information-Theoretic Baselines under Massart Noise ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise"), this establishes the
information-theoretic baseline of Θ~​((d​Δ)1/2)\tilde{\Theta}((d\Delta)^{1/2}) for the excess
error under both the Massart noise
and RCN, provided 1/2−η1/2-\eta can be treated as a
positive constant (the primary case for which these noise models
are studied).

###### Theorem 2.2(Information-Theoretic Lower Bound).

For every T>0T>0 and Δ∈(0,1)\Delta\in(0,1), there is a family of instances of learning halfspaces with η\eta-RCN such that provided (1−2​η)3>d​Δ(1-2\eta)^{3}>d\Delta, there is no learning algorithm 𝒜\mathcal{A} that can achieve error err(T)​(𝒜)≤optT+o​(d​Δ/(1−2​η))\mathrm{err}^{(T)}(\mathcal{A})\leq\mathrm{opt}\_{T}+o(\sqrt{d\Delta/(1-2\eta)}), with probability 1/21/2 for every instance in the family.

## 3 Efficiently Learning Drifted Halfspaces with Massart Noise

In this section, we establish [Theorem˜1.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#S1.Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 1.1 (Main Algorithmic Result). ‣ 1.1 Our Results ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise").

Our main learning algorithm, [Algorithm˜1](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#alg1 "In 3 Efficiently Learning Drifted Halfspaces with Massart Noise ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise"), works in epochs, where the iith epoch corresponds to time steps t∈{(i−1)W+1,…,iWt\in\{(i-1)W+1,\dots,iW}.
In epoch ii, [Algorithm˜1](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#alg1 "In 3 Efficiently Learning Drifted Halfspaces with Massart Noise ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise") maintains a hypothesis h^(i)\hat{h}^{(i)} for prediction and collects a set of examples S(i)S^{(i)} observed during this epoch. At the end of the epoch, [Algorithm˜1](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#alg1 "In 3 Efficiently Learning Drifted Halfspaces with Massart Noise ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise") updates the hypothesis h(i)h^{(i)} using the collected dataset S(i)S^{(i)} through a subroutine in [Algorithm˜2](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#alg2 "In 3 Efficiently Learning Drifted Halfspaces with Massart Noise ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise").

Algorithm 1DriftedMassart (Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise)

1:Input: noise level η∈(0,1/2),\eta\in(0,1/2), margin parameter γ∈(0,1),\gamma\in(0,1), drift rate Δ>0\Delta>0

2:Output: A hypothesis hh for each round tt.

3:Let W=2​⌊Δ−2/3​log⁡((Δ)−1)⌋,i=1W=2\lfloor\Delta^{-2/3}\log((\Delta)^{-1})\rfloor,i=1, h^0​(x)≡1\hat{h}^{0}(x)\equiv 1

4:fort=1,2,…t=1,2,\dotsdo

5:  Output hypothesis h^(i−1)\hat{h}^{(i-1)} and receive (x(t),y(t))(x^{(t)},y^{(t)})

6:S(i)←S(i)∪{(x(t),y(t))}S^{(i)}\leftarrow S^{(i)}\cup\{(x^{(t)},y^{(t)})\}

7:ift=i​Wt=iWthen

8:h^(i)←DriftPerceptron​(η,γ,S(i),γ/W)\hat{h}^{(i)}\leftarrow\textsc{DriftPerceptron}(\eta,\gamma,S^{(i)},\gamma/\sqrt{W}), S(i)←∅S^{(i)}\leftarrow\emptyset, i←i+1i\leftarrow i+1

[Algorithm˜2](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#alg2 "In 3 Efficiently Learning Drifted Halfspaces with Massart Noise ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise") equally partitions S(i)S^{(i)} into sets S1(i)S\_{1}^{(i)} and S2(i)S\_{2}^{(i)}. Set S1(i)S\_{1}^{(i)}, consisting of the first half of the examples, is used to update the parameter vector w(i)w^{(i)}, while the second half of the examples in S2(i)S\_{2}^{(i)} are used to select a good hypothesis halfspace hw(i)h\_{w^{(i)}} from the trajectory of updates.

Algorithm 2DriftPerceptron (Subroutine for Learning a Single Halfspace over a Dataset)

1:Input: noise level η∈(0,1/2)\eta\in(0,1/2), margin parameter γ∈(0,1)\gamma\in(0,1), step size μ>0\mu>0, a dataset S={(x(i),y(i))}i=12​mS=\{(x^{(i)},y^{(i)})\}\_{i=1}^{2m} of 2​m2m labeled examples

2:Output:h^=sign​(w^⋅x):ℝd→{±1}\hat{h}=\mathrm{sign}(\hat{w}\cdot x):\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\{\pm 1\}

3:w(0)=e1w^{(0)}=e\_{1}

4:Split S=S1∪S2S=S\_{1}\cup S\_{2}, where S1={(x(i),y(i))}i=1m,S2=S∖S1S\_{1}=\{(x^{(i)},y^{(i)})\}\_{i=1}^{m},S\_{2}=S\setminus S\_{1}

5:fori=1,2,…,mi=1,2,\dots,mdo

6:g​(w(i);x,y)←((1−2​η)​sign​(w(i)⋅x)−y)​x/max⁡{\|w(i)⋅x\|,γ}g(w^{(i)};x,y)\leftarrow((1-2\eta)\mathrm{sign}(w^{(i)}\cdot x)-y)x/\max\{\left\|w^{(i)}\cdot x\right\|,\gamma\}

7:w(i+1)←proj𝔹​(1)​(w(i)−μ​gt​(w(i),x,y))w^{(i+1)}\leftarrow\mathrm{proj}\_{\mathbb{B}(1)}(w^{(i)}-\mu g\_{t}(w^{(i)},x,y)), h^i+1​(x)=sign​(w(i+1)⋅x)\hat{h}\_{i+1}(x)=\mathrm{sign}(w^{(i+1)}\cdot x)

8:Return h^=argminh^ierr^​(h^i)\hat{h}=\operatorname\*{argmin}\_{\hat{h}\_{i}}\hat{\mathrm{err}}(\hat{h}\_{i}), where err^​(h^i)=1\|S2\|​∑(x,y)∈S2𝟙​{h^i​(x)≠y}\hat{\mathrm{err}}(\hat{h}\_{i})=\frac{1}{\|S\_{2}\|}\sum\_{(x,y)\in S\_{2}}\mathds{1}\{\hat{h}\_{i}(x)\neq y\}.

In each round, we feed [Algorithm˜2](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#alg2 "In 3 Efficiently Learning Drifted Halfspaces with Massart Noise ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise") an example (x,y)(x,y) and
generate a “gradient” vector g​(w(i);x,y)g(w^{(i)};x,y) based on the current vector w(i)∈𝕊d−1w^{(i)}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1} and the example (x,y)(x,y). With such a gradient vector, we modify w(i)w^{(i)} by running a single step projected gradient descent with a carefully chosen step size. By standard regret analysis, we get the following fact, the proof of which is deferred to [Section˜C.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#A3.SS1 "C.1 Proof of ˜3.1 ‣ Appendix C Omitted Proofs from Section˜3 ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise").

###### Fact 3.1(Progress Measure Measurement).

Let t>0t>0 be any time step and let
S={(x(i),y(i))}i=1mS=\{(x^{(i)},y^{(i)})\}\_{i=1}^{m} be such that (x(i),y(i))∼D(t+i)(x^{(i)},y^{(i)})\sim D^{(t+i)}. For T∈\[m\]T\in\[m\] and w∗∈𝕊d−1w^{\*}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}, [Algorithm˜2](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#alg2 "In 3 Efficiently Learning Drifted Halfspaces with Massart Noise ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise") satisfies

|     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  | 1T​∑i=1T𝐄(x,y)∼D(t+i)\[g​(w(i);x,y)⋅(w(i)−w∗)\]≤R​(T,μ,γ),\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\sum\_{i=1}^{T}\operatorname\*{\mathbf{E}}\_{(x,y)\sim D^{(t+i)}}\[g(w^{(i)};x,y)\cdot(w^{(i)}-w^{\*})\]\leq R(T,\mu,\gamma), |  |

where R​(T,μ,γ)=1/(2​T​μ)+2​μ/γ2−∑i=1Tξi/TR(T,\mu,\gamma)=1/(2T\mu)+2\mu/\gamma^{2}-\sum\_{i=1}^{T}\xi\_{i}/T, ξi:=(g​(w(i);x,y)−𝐄(x,y)∼D(t+i)g​(w(i);x,y))⋅(w(i)−w∗)\xi\_{i}:=\left(g(w^{(i)};x,y)-\operatorname\*{\mathbf{E}}\_{(x,y)\sim D^{(t+i)}}g(w^{(i)};x,y)\right)\cdot(w^{(i)}-w^{\*}).

Here, the term 1/(2​T​μ)+2​μ/γ21/(2T\mu)+2\mu/\gamma^{2} can be interpreted as “optimization error” and the term ∑i=1Tξi/T\sum\_{i=1}^{T}\xi\_{i}/T as “concentration error”.
We remark that the proof of [˜3.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#S3.Thmfact1 "Fact 3.1 (Progress Measure Measurement). ‣ 3 Efficiently Learning Drifted Halfspaces with Massart Noise ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise") only relies on the boundedness of the gradient used in each round. To make use of [˜3.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#S3.Thmfact1 "Fact 3.1 (Progress Measure Measurement). ‣ 3 Efficiently Learning Drifted Halfspaces with Massart Noise ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise") to analyze the performance of the halfspace hw(i)h\_{w^{(i)}} in the update trajectory, we need to choose a suitable gradient g​(w;x,y)g(w;x,y) that can relate the expected regret term 𝐄(x,y)∼D(t+i)\[g​(w(i);x,y)⋅(w(i)−w∗)\]\operatorname\*{\mathbf{E}}\_{(x,y)\sim D^{(t+i)}}\[g(w^{(i)};x,y)\cdot(w^{(i)}-w^{\*})\] to the quantity of interest—the prediction error.

Our choice of the gradient vector is
inspired by \[ [DKTZ25](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx21 "")\], which considers an online learning problem where labels are generated according to a _fixed_ halfspace with Massart noise.
The choice of g​(w(i);x,y)g(w^{(i)};x,y) can be seen as the gradient of the leaky ReLU function ℓη​(w;x,y)=((1−2​η)​\|y​w⋅x\|+y​w⋅x)\ell\_{\eta}(w;x,y)=((1-2\eta)\left\|yw\cdot x\right\|+yw\cdot x) weighted by max{\|w(i)⋅x\|,γ}−1\max\{\left\|w^{(i)}\cdot x\right\|,\gamma\}^{-1}, where the chosen weight is used to balance penalizations for misclassified examples with different margins.

Although the choice of gradient is similar, the analysis from prior work does not apply to the setting where the distribution drifts. The main difficulty is that under drifting distribution over the labeled examples, there is no single ground truth halfspace hw∗h\_{w^{\*}} that can realize all distributions D(i)D^{(i)}, i≥0i\geq 0. Denote by (w∗)(i)(w^{\*})^{(i)} the parameter vector of the halfspace that realizes D(i)D^{(i)}. When the marginal distribution is a uniform distribution over the unit sphere, then prior work \[ [CMEDV10](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx12 ""), [HKY15](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx26 "")\] shows that if dT​V​(D(i),D(i+1))d\_{\mathrm{T}V}(D^{(i)},D^{(i+1)}) is small, then ‖(w∗)(i)−(w∗)(i+1)‖\left\\|(w^{\*})^{(i)}-(w^{\*})^{(i+1)}\right\\| is also small, which makes it possible to treat (w∗)(i)(w^{\*})^{(i)} as approximately unchanged.
However, when such a distributional assumption is removed, dT​V​(D(i),D(i+1))d\_{\mathrm{T}V}(D^{(i)},D^{(i+1)}) may be small, but the geometric distance between (w∗)(i)(w^{\*})^{(i)} and (w∗)(i+1)(w^{\*})^{(i+1)} can still be quite large. We bypass the difficulty of analyzing the change of ‖(w∗)(i)−(w∗)(i+1)‖\left\\|(w^{\*})^{(i)}-(w^{\*})^{(i+1)}\right\\| by showing that despite ‖(w∗)(i)−(w∗)(i+1)‖\left\\|(w^{\*})^{(i)}-(w^{\*})^{(i+1)}\right\\| being potentially large, the prediction error in each round can always be controlled by the expected regret term plus a drift-controlled error term. This technical approach is summarized in our main technical lemma, stated below.

###### Lemma 3.1(Regret to Error).

Let i,m∈ℕi,m\in\mathbb{N} and let w∗∈𝕊d−1w^{\*}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1} be a vector that realizes D(i+m)D^{(i+m)}. There exists a function Fi​(w∗,w,x):𝕊d−1×𝕊d−1×𝕊d−1→ℝF\_{i}(w^{\*},w,x):\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\to\mathbb{R} such that
for any x∈𝕊d−1x\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1} and for any w∈𝕊d−1w\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1},

|     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  | 𝐄y∼D(i)∣x\[g​(w;x,y)⋅(w−w∗)\]≥2​(err(i)​(w,x)−η)−Fi.\displaystyle\operatorname\*{\mathbf{E}}\_{y\sim D^{(i)}\mid x}\[g(w;x,y)\cdot(w-w^{\*})\]\;\geq 2(\mathrm{err}^{(i)}(w,x)-\eta)-F\_{i}. |  |

Additionally, \|Fi\|≤1/γ\left\|F\_{i}\right\|\leq 1/\gamma, and for every w∈𝕊d−1w\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}, 𝐄x∼Dx(i)Fi​(w∗,w,x)=O​(Δ​m/γ)\operatorname\*{\mathbf{E}}\_{x\sim D^{(i)}\_{x}}F\_{i}(w^{\*},w,x)=O(\Delta m/\gamma).

We give an overview of the proof of [Lemma˜3.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#S3.Thmlemma1 "Lemma 3.1 (Regret to Error). ‣ 3 Efficiently Learning Drifted Halfspaces with Massart Noise ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise"), deferring the full details of the proof to [Section˜C.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#A3.SS2 "C.2 Omitted Proofs for Lemma˜3.1 ‣ Appendix C Omitted Proofs from Section˜3 ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise").

The proof of [Lemma˜3.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#S3.Thmlemma1 "Lemma 3.1 (Regret to Error). ‣ 3 Efficiently Learning Drifted Halfspaces with Massart Noise ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise") is divided into two cases based on whether \|w⋅x\|>γ\|w\cdot x\|>\gamma. For each case CC, we construct a bounded function Fi(C)​(w∗,w,x)F^{(C)}\_{i}(w^{\*},w,x) such that
𝐄y∼D(i)∣x\[g​(w;x,y)⋅(w−w∗)\]≥2​(err(i)​(w,x)−η)−Fi(C)​(w∗,w,x)\operatorname\*{\mathbf{E}}\_{y\sim D^{(i)}\mid x}\[g(w;x,y)\cdot(w-w^{\*})\]\geq 2(\mathrm{err}^{(i)}(w,x)-\eta)-F^{(C)}\_{i}(w^{\*},w,x)
holds deterministically for every x∈𝕊d−1∩Cx\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\cap C and 𝐄x∼Dx(i)\[Fi(C)​(w∗,w,x)​𝟙​{C}\]=O​(Δ​m/γ)\operatorname\*{\mathbf{E}}\_{x\sim D^{(i)}\_{x}}\[F^{(C)}\_{i}(w^{\*},w,x)\mathds{1}\{C\}\]=O(\Delta m/\gamma). Given this construction, the desired function FiF\_{i} can be taken as the summation of Fi(C)​(w∗,w,x)​𝟙​{C}F^{(C)}\_{i}(w^{\*},w,x)\mathds{1}\{C\}.

The central technical part of the proof is to show that the expectation of the constructed function FiF\_{i}, which we call “drift error”, is essentially controlled by the amount of distribution drift m​Δm\Delta. Our construction of FiF\_{i} enables us to relate the expectation to the change of the disagreement region {x∣(w∗⋅x)​((w∗)(i)⋅x)<0}\{x\mid(w^{\*}\cdot x)((w^{\*})^{(i)}\cdot x)<0\} as well as the change of the noise rate ηi​(x)−ηi+m​(x)\eta\_{i}(x)-\eta\_{i+m}(x). We establish the following two claims in order to bound the expectation of Fi(C)F^{(C)}\_{i}, whose proofs are deferred to [Section˜C.2.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#A3.SS2.SSS3 "C.2.3 Proof of ˜3.1 ‣ C.2 Omitted Proofs for Lemma˜3.1 ‣ Appendix C Omitted Proofs from Section˜3 ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise") and [Section˜C.2.4](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#A3.SS2.SSS4 "C.2.4 Proof of ˜3.2 ‣ C.2 Omitted Proofs for Lemma˜3.1 ‣ Appendix C Omitted Proofs from Section˜3 ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise").

###### Claim 3.1.

For i,m∈Ni,\,m\in N, let D(i),D(i+m)D^{(i)},\,D^{(i+m)} be realized by ((w∗)(i),ηi​(x))((w^{\*})^{(i)},\,\eta\_{i}(x)) and (w∗,ηm+i​(x))(w^{\*},\,\eta\_{m+i}(x)). Then 𝐏𝐫x∼Dx(i)\[ϕ​(w∗⋅x,(w∗)(i)⋅x)=−1\]=O​(Δ​m1−2​η)\operatorname\*{\mathbf{Pr}}\_{x\sim D^{(i)}\_{x}}\[\phi(w^{\*}\cdot x,(w^{\*})^{(i)}\cdot x)=-1\]=O\big(\frac{\Delta m}{1-2\eta}\big).

###### Claim 3.2.

For i,m∈Ni,\,m\in N, let D(i),D(i+m)D^{(i)},\,D^{(i+m)} be realized by ((w∗)(i),ηi​(x))((w^{\*})^{(i)},\eta\_{i}(x)) and (w∗,ηm+i​(x))(w^{\*},\eta\_{m+i}(x)). Let ℰ:={x:ϕ​(w∗⋅x,(w∗)(i)⋅x)=1,ηi​(x)≥ηm+i​(x)}\mathcal{E}:=\{x:\phi(w^{\*}\cdot x,(w^{\*})^{(i)}\cdot x)=1,\,\eta\_{i}(x)\geq\eta\_{m+i}(x)\}. Then 𝐄x∼Dx(i)\[(ηi(x)−ηm+i(x)𝟙{ℰ}\]=O(Δm)\operatorname\*{\mathbf{E}}\_{x\sim D^{(i)}\_{x}}\[(\eta\_{i}(x)-\eta\_{m+i}(x)\mathds{1}\{\mathcal{E}\}\]=O(\Delta m).

[Lemma˜3.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#S3.Thmlemma1 "Lemma 3.1 (Regret to Error). ‣ 3 Efficiently Learning Drifted Halfspaces with Massart Noise ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise") shows that the excess error of a hypothesis in each round can be bounded by its “average regret” plus a contribution due to the drift error. Thus, to prove [Theorem˜1.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#S1.Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 1.1 (Main Algorithmic Result). ‣ 1.1 Our Results ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise"), we only need to show that the “average regret” can be bounded by O​(Δ1/3/γ)O(\Delta^{1/3}/\gamma), while controlling the drift error contribution.
By [˜3.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#S3.Thmfact1 "Fact 3.1 (Progress Measure Measurement). ‣ 3 Efficiently Learning Drifted Halfspaces with Massart Noise ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise") and [Lemma˜3.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#S3.Thmlemma1 "Lemma 3.1 (Regret to Error). ‣ 3 Efficiently Learning Drifted Halfspaces with Massart Noise ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise"), we know that we need to control the optimization error 1/(2​T​μ)+2​μ/γ21/(2T\mu)+2\mu/\gamma^{2}, the drift error O​(T​Δ/γ)O(T\Delta/\gamma), and the concentration error ∑i=1Tξi\sum\_{i=1}^{T}\xi\_{i}. The first two errors are minimized by carefully choosing the epoch length WW as well as the update step size μ\mu, while the concentration term is controlled by the following lemma.

###### Lemma 3.2.

For t,i>0t,i>0 and w∗∈𝕊d−1w^{\*}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1},
define ξi:=(g​(w(i);x,y)−𝐄(x,y)∼D(t+i)g​(w(i);x,y))⋅(w(i)−w∗)\xi\_{i}:=\left(g(w^{(i)};x,y)-\operatorname\*{\mathbf{E}}\_{(x,y)\sim D^{(t+i)}}g(w^{(i)};x,y)\right)\cdot(w^{(i)}-w^{\*}), (x,y)∼D(t+i)(x,y)\sim D^{(t+i)}. For every T>0T>0, with probability at least 1−δ/21-\delta/2, it holds ∑i=1Tξi≤γ−1​T​log⁡(1/δ).\sum\_{i=1}^{T}\xi\_{i}\leq\gamma^{-1}\sqrt{T}\log(1/\delta).

###### Proof of [Theorem˜1.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1\#S1.Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 1.1 (Main Algorithmic Result). ‣ 1.1 Our Results ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise").

To prove the correctness of [Algorithm˜1](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#alg1 "In 3 Efficiently Learning Drifted Halfspaces with Massart Noise ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise"), we show that for t=ℓ​Wt=\ell W, with probability 1−δ1-\delta, err(t)​(h(ℓ))≤η+O~​(Δ1/3/γ)\mathrm{err}^{(t)}(h^{(\ell)})\leq\eta+\tilde{O}(\Delta^{1/3}/\gamma). Suppose this is correct, then for t′=ℓ​W+jt^{\prime}=\ell W+j, 1≤j≤W1\leq j\leq W, we have with probability at least 1−δ1-\delta,

|     |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  | err(t′)​(h(ℓ))\displaystyle\mathrm{err}^{(t^{\prime})}(h^{(\ell)}) | ≤𝐏𝐫(x,y)∼D(i​W)\[h(ℓ)​(x)≠y\]+j​Δ\displaystyle\leq\operatorname\*{\mathbf{Pr}}\_{(x,y)\sim D^{(iW)}}\[h^{(\ell)}(x)\neq y\]+j\Delta |  |
|  |  | ≤η+O~​(Δ1/3/γ).\displaystyle\leq\eta+\tilde{O}(\Delta^{1/3}/\gamma). |  |

To bound above the error of err(t)​(h(ℓ))\mathrm{err}^{(t)}(h^{(\ell)}) for t=ℓ​Wt=\ell W, we apply [˜3.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#S3.Thmfact1 "Fact 3.1 (Progress Measure Measurement). ‣ 3 Efficiently Learning Drifted Halfspaces with Massart Noise ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise") with t=ℓ​Wt=\ell W and w∗∈𝕊d−1w^{\*}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}, the vector that realizes D(t+T)D^{(t+T)} with T=W/2T=W/2 and apply [Lemma˜3.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#S3.Thmlemma1 "Lemma 3.1 (Regret to Error). ‣ 3 Efficiently Learning Drifted Halfspaces with Massart Noise ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise") with i=t+ji=t+j
and m=T−jm=T-j for each j∈\[T\]j\in\[T\].
We have with probability at least 0.950.95

|     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  | 1T​∑j=1T(err(t+j)​(w(j))−η)\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\sum\_{j=1}^{T}\left(\mathrm{err}^{(t+j)}(w^{(j)})-\eta\right) |  |
|  | ≤1T​∑j=1T𝐄(x,y)∼D(t+j)\[g​(w(j);x,y)⋅(w(j)−w∗)\]+O​(T​Δ/γ)\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{T}\sum\_{j=1}^{T}\operatorname\*{\mathbf{E}}\_{(x,y)\sim D^{(t+j)}}\[g(w^{(j)};x,y)\cdot(w^{(j)}-w^{\*})\]+O(T\Delta/\gamma) |  |
|  | ≤12​T​μ+μ2​γ2−1T​∑j=1Tξj+O​(T​Δ/γ)\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2T\mu}+\frac{\mu}{2\gamma^{2}}-\frac{1}{T}\sum\_{j=1}^{T}\xi\_{j}+O(T\Delta/\gamma) |  |
|  | ≤1/(T​γ)+O​(T​Δ/γ)−1T​∑j=1Tξj\displaystyle\leq 1/(\sqrt{T}\gamma)+O(T\Delta/\gamma)-\frac{1}{T}\sum\_{j=1}^{T}\xi\_{j} |  |
|  | ≤1/(T​γ)+O​(T​Δ/γ)+O​(1/(T​γ))=O~​(Δ1/3/γ).\displaystyle\leq 1/(\sqrt{T}\gamma)+O(T\Delta/\gamma)+O(1/(\sqrt{T}\gamma))=\tilde{O}(\Delta^{1/3}/\gamma). |  |

Here, the third inequality follows from the choice μ=γ/T\mu=\gamma/\sqrt{T}, the forth inequality follows from [Lemma˜3.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#S3.Thmlemma2 "Lemma 3.2. ‣ 3 Efficiently Learning Drifted Halfspaces with Massart Noise ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise") and the last inequality follows from the choice of T=Θ~​(Δ−2/3)T=\tilde{\Theta}(\Delta^{-2/3}).
This implies that with probability 0.950.95 there is at least one j∈\[T\]j\in\[T\] such that , err(t+j)​(w(j))≤η+O~​(Δ1/3/γ)\mathrm{err}^{(t+j)}(w^{(j)})\leq\eta+\tilde{O}(\Delta^{1/3}/\gamma), as minj∈\[T\]⁡(err(t+j)​(w(j))−η)≤1T​∑j=1T(err(t+j)​(w(j))−η)\min\_{j\in\[T\]}\left(\mathrm{err}^{(t+j)}(w^{(j)})-\eta\right)\leq\frac{1}{T}\sum\_{j=1}^{T}\left(\mathrm{err}^{(t+j)}(w^{(j)})-\eta\right).

We next argue that we are able to select a good hypothesis by looking at the empirical error.
For each j∈\[T\]j\in\[T\], consider the halfspace h^j=hw(j)\hat{h}\_{j}=h\_{w^{(j)}}. By the assumption on the drift rate, we have \|err((ℓ+1)​W)​(h^j)−err(t+t′)​(h^j)\|≤Δ​W=Δ1/3\big\|{\mathrm{err}^{((\ell+1)W)}(\hat{h}\_{j})-\mathrm{err}^{(t+t^{\prime})}(\hat{h}\_{j})}\big\|\leq\Delta W=\Delta^{1/3} for every t′∈\[W/2\]t^{\prime}\in\[W/2\], which implies that

|     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  | \|err((ℓ+1)​W)​(h^j)−2W​∑ℓ=1W/2err(t+t′+W/2)​(h^j)\|≤Δ​W.\displaystyle\Big\|\mathrm{err}^{((\ell+1)W)}(\hat{h}\_{j})-\frac{2}{W}\sum\_{\ell=1}^{W/2}\mathrm{err}^{(t+t^{\prime}+W/2)}(\hat{h}\_{j})\Big\|\leq\Delta W. |  |

Recall the definition of err^​(h^j)\hat{\mathrm{err}}(\hat{h}\_{j}) from [Algorithm˜2](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#alg2 "In 3 Efficiently Learning Drifted Halfspaces with Massart Noise ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise") as the empirical error of h^j\hat{h}\_{j} over an independent set S2(i)S\_{2}^{(i)}. By Hoeffding’s inequality,
we have

|     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  | 𝐏𝐫\[err^​(h^j)−2W​∑ℓ=1W/2err(t+ℓ+W/2)​(h^j)>Δ1/3\]≤0.05W.\displaystyle\operatorname\*{\mathbf{Pr}}\Big\[\hat{\mathrm{err}}(\hat{h}\_{j})-\frac{2}{W}\sum\_{\ell=1}^{W/2}\mathrm{err}^{(t+\ell+W/2)}(\hat{h}\_{j})>\Delta^{1/3}\Big\]\leq\frac{0.05}{W}. |  |

Thus, by the union bound, with probability at least 0.950.95,
we have \|err^​(h^j)−err((ℓ+1)​W)​(h^j)\|≤Δ1/3\big\|{\hat{\mathrm{err}}(\hat{h}\_{j})-\mathrm{err}^{((\ell+1)W)}(\hat{h}\_{j})}\big\|\leq\Delta^{1/3} for j∈\[T\]j\in\[T\].
Since with probability 0.950.95, there is some j∗∈\[T\]j^{\*}\in\[T\] such that err(t+j∗)​(w(j∗))≤η+O~​(Δ1/3/γ)\mathrm{err}^{(t+j^{\*})}(w^{(j^{\*})})\leq\eta+\tilde{O}(\Delta^{1/3}/\gamma), we know that err((ℓ+1)​W)​(w(j∗))≤η+O~​(Δ1/3/γ)\mathrm{err}^{((\ell+1)W)}(w^{(j^{\*})})\leq\eta+\tilde{O}(\Delta^{1/3}/\gamma). This implies that the selected hypothesis that minimizes err^​(hj)\hat{\mathrm{err}}(h\_{j}) must have err((ℓ+1)​W)​(h^)≤η+O~​(Δ1/3/γ)\mathrm{err}^{((\ell+1)W)}(\hat{h})\leq\eta+\tilde{O}(\Delta^{1/3}/\gamma), with probability 0.90.9.
∎

##### Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with RCN

Under the Massart noise condition, even without distribution drift, it is computationally hard \[ [NT22](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx38 "")\] to output a hypothesis with error less than η−o​(1)\eta-o(1). For the special case of RCN, although optt\mathrm{opt}\_{t} can change over time due to the distribution drift, we show that within each epoch, there is some η∗\eta^{\*} such that for every time step tt within the epoch, optt≤η∗+O​(Δ​W)\mathrm{opt}\_{t}\leq\eta^{\*}+O(\Delta W). Thus, by repeatedly running [Algorithm˜1](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#alg1 "In 3 Efficiently Learning Drifted Halfspaces with Massart Noise ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise") with different choices of η\eta, we are able to get error optt+O~​(Δ1/3/γ)\mathrm{opt}\_{t}+\tilde{O}(\Delta^{1/3}/\gamma). Specifically, we show (see [Section˜C.4](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#A3.SS4 "C.4 Omitted Details for Learning Drifting Halfspace under RCN ‣ Appendix C Omitted Proofs from Section˜3 ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise") for the proof).

###### Theorem 3.1(Efficient Drift Learning with RCN).

Consider the problem of learning drifting halfspaces with η\eta-RCN. There is an algorithm 𝒜\mathcal{A} such that for any time step T=Ω~​(Δ−2/3)T=\tilde{\Omega}(\Delta^{-2/3}), 𝒜\mathcal{A} runs in poly​(d,1/γ,Δ)\mathrm{poly}(d,1/\gamma,\Delta) time and outputs a hypothesis h^(T)\hat{h}^{(T)} such that with probability at least 9/109/10, err(T)​(h^(T))≤optT+O~​(Δ1/3/γ)\mathrm{err}^{(T)}(\hat{h}^{(T)})\leq\mathrm{opt}\_{T}+\tilde{O}(\Delta^{1/3}/\gamma).

##### Efficiently Learning Realizable Drifting Halfspaces

Another application of our algorithmic framework is an efficient algorithm for drifting halfspaces in the realizable setting with an improved error guarantee. When the marginal distribution is uniform over the unit sphere in ℝd\mathbb{R}^{d}, \[ [HKY15](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx26 "")\] shows that it is possible to efficiently achieve an error of d​Δ\sqrt{d\Delta}. However, when the strong distributional assumption is removed, the only known efficient learning algorithm with a provable error guarantee d​Δd\sqrt{\Delta} is designed by \[ [HL94](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx27 "")\]. Such an algorithm uses a simple observation that if the epoch length is W=O​(Δ−1/2)W=O(\Delta^{-1/2}), then in the realizable setting, with constant probability, all observed examples within the epoch are consistent with a single halfspace and thus empirical risk minimization can be efficiently implemented. In the case of learning a γ\gamma-margin halfspace, such a trick only gives an error of O​(Δ​γ−2)O(\sqrt{\Delta}\gamma^{-2}) through a standard generalization analysis, because the sample size used to update the hypothesis is too small to achieve a good generalization error. To overcome such a difficulty, we increase the length of an epoch to (γ​Δ)−1/2(\gamma\Delta)^{-1/2}. Although within such a larger epoch, empirical risk minimization is no longer efficiently implementable, because the labels are no longer consistent with a single halfspace
, our algorithm uses a carefully designed projected gradient descent to get a better generalization error O~​(Δ​γ−3/2)\tilde{O}(\sqrt{\Delta}\gamma^{-3/2}).

###### Theorem 3.2(Efficient Drift Learning in Realizable Case).

Consider the problem of learning drifting halfspaces. There is an algorithm 𝒜\mathcal{A} such that for any time step T=Ω~​((γ​Δ)−1/2)T=\tilde{\Omega}((\gamma\Delta)^{-1/2}), 𝒜\mathcal{A} runs in poly​(d,1/γ,Δ)\mathrm{poly}(d,1/\gamma,\Delta) time and outputs a hypothesis h^(T)\hat{h}^{(T)} such that with probability at least 9/109/10, err(T)​(h^(T))=O~​(Δ​γ−3/2)\mathrm{err}^{(T)}(\hat{h}^{(T)})=\tilde{O}(\sqrt{\Delta}\gamma^{-3/2}).

Compared to our Massart [Algorithm˜1](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#alg1 "In 3 Efficiently Learning Drifted Halfspaces with Massart Noise ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise"),
there are differences in the design and analysis of the learner in the realizable setting. Without label noise, we do not weight the gradient by max{\|w(i)⋅x\|,γ}−1\max\{\left\|w^{(i)}\cdot x\right\|,\gamma\}^{-1} and use g​(w,x,y)←(sign​(w⋅x)−y)​xg(w,x,y)\leftarrow(\mathrm{sign}(w\cdot x)-y)x instead. Such a choice has the property that 𝐄(x,y)∼D(i)‖g​(w;x,y)‖2=err(i)​(hw)\operatorname\*{\mathbf{E}}\_{(x,y)\sim D^{(i)}}\left\\|g(w;x,y)\right\\|^{2}=\mathrm{err}^{(i)}(h\_{w}). This allows for
a tighter regret bound as well as better control of drift error, and results in a better error guarantee of order Δ1/2\Delta^{1/2} instead of Δ1/3\Delta^{1/3}. See [Section˜C.5](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#A3.SS5 "C.5 Proof of Theorem˜3.2 ‣ Appendix C Omitted Proofs from Section˜3 ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise") for the formal details.

## 4 Low-Degree Polynomial Hardness

In this section, we establish our information-computation tradeoff
for learning drifting halfspaces with RCN, thereby establishing
[Theorem˜1.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#S1.Thmtheorem2 "Theorem 1.2 (Informal Implication of Theorem˜4.1). ‣ 1.1 Our Results ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise").

To establish the hardness result, we first introduce the following testing problem, which we will show later can be solved by a learning algorithm with a small excess error.

###### Definition 4.1(Trajectory Testing Problem).

A trajectory testing problem ℬ​(D0,𝒟)\mathcal{B}(D^{0},\mathcal{D}) is defined by a distribution D(0)D^{(0)} and a family of distributions 𝒟\mathcal{D} over Z=(ℝd×{±1})TZ=(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\{\pm 1\})^{T}. An algorithm is given a sample z=(z1,…,zT)∼D,zi=(xi,yi),i∈\[T\],z=(z\_{1},\dots,z\_{T})\sim D,z\_{i}=(x\_{i},y\_{i}),i\in\[T\], and is asked to distinguish whether D=D(0)D=D^{(0)} or if DD is a distribution drawn uniformly from 𝒟\mathcal{D}. In particular, this is the following hypothesis testing problem:

1. 1.


Null hypothesisH0H\_{0}: All labeled examples zi,z\_{i},i∈\[T\],i\in\[T\], are drawn from the same distribution: Di(0)=Dj(0),D^{(0)}\_{i}=D\_{j}^{(0)},∀i,j∈\[T\].\forall i,j\in\[T\]. Furthermore, for each i∈\[T\]i\in\[T\], yiy\_{i} is independent of xix\_{i} and such that 𝐏𝐫Di(0)(yi=1)=η∈(0,1/2)\operatorname\*{\mathbf{Pr}}\_{D^{(0)}\_{i}}(y\_{i}=1)=\eta\in(0,1/2).

2. 2.


Alternative hypothesisH1H\_{1}: Labeled examples satisfy zi∼Diz\_{i}\sim D\_{i}, where D∈𝒟D\in\mathcal{D} and each distribution DiD\_{i} corresponds to an instance of learning γ\gamma-margin halfspaces under RCN with noise rate ηi≤η∈(0,1)\eta\_{i}\leq\eta\in(0,1). Furthermore, for every i∈\[T\]i\in\[T\], dT​V​(Di,Di+1)≤Δ.d\_{\mathrm{T}V}(D\_{i},D\_{i+1})\leq\Delta.


To establish our computational hardness result, we need to formally define
the computational model. We restrict the learning algorithms to the family of low-degree polynomial estimators, one of the most commonly used algorithmic families in statistics and machine learning literature \[ [Wei25](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx42 "")\].

More formally,
Let z=(z1,…,zm)∈ℝd×mz=(z\_{1},\dots,z\_{m})\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times m} be an input sample. A degree kk polynomial algorithm 𝒜\mathcal{A} specifies a function f​(z)=(f1​(z),…,fd​(z)):ℝd×m→ℝdf(z)=(f\_{1}(z),\dots,f\_{d}(z)):\mathbb{R}^{d\times m}\to\mathbb{R}^{d} such that for i∈\[d\]i\in\[d\], fi​(z)f\_{i}(z) is a polynomial over zz with degree at most kk. We say ff has a samplewise degree (ℓ,k)(\ell,k) if ff can be written as a linear combination of monomials such that each monomial has a degree ℓ\ell at each ziz\_{i} and has non-zero degree for at most kk examples.

###### Definition 4.2(Low-Degree Polynomial Test under Concept Drift).

Under [Definition˜4.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#S4.Thmdefinition1 "Definition 4.1 (Trajectory Testing Problem). ‣ 4 Low-Degree Polynomial Hardness ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise"), let p:ℝ(d+1)×T→ℝp:\mathbb{R}^{(d+1)\times T}\to\mathbb{R} be a polynomial over ZZ. A low degree polynomial test using pp with threshold tt is an algorithm such that given a sample zz, it returns H0H\_{0} if p​(z)>tp(z)>t. Furthermore, we say the polynomial pp is an ϵ\epsilon-distinguisher for [Definition˜4.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#S4.Thmdefinition1 "Definition 4.1 (Trajectory Testing Problem). ‣ 4 Low-Degree Polynomial Hardness ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise") if

|     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  | \|𝐄z∼D(0)p​(z)−𝐄D∼𝒟𝐄z∼D⁡p​(z)\|≥ϵ​𝐕𝐚𝐫z∼D(0)​p​(z).\displaystyle\big\|{\operatorname\*{\mathbf{E}}\_{z\sim D^{(0)}}p(z)-\operatorname\*{\mathbf{E}}\_{D\sim\mathcal{D}}\operatorname\*{\mathbf{E}}\_{z\sim D}p(z)}\big\|\geq\epsilon\sqrt{\mathbf{Var}\_{z\sim D^{(0)}}p(z)}. |  |

Intuitively, at a given time step TT, an algorithm is given the history of observed examples and is allowed to compute any polynomial function over the trajectory of the examples. If a polynomial pp is not an ϵ\epsilon-distinguisher for the testing problem, then for a trajectory zz sampled under H0H\_{0} or H1H\_{1}, the result of p​(z)p(z) will have difference about ϵ\epsilon with constant probability and thus is not reliable to distinguish the two cases. In particular, evaluating a _general_ degree kk polynomial over ℝ(d+1)×T\mathbb{R}^{(d+1)\times T} requires time (d​T)k(dT)^{k}, which implies that if we can rule out super-constant degree polynomials as 11-distinguishers, we can provide evidence of the computational hardness of the problem.

##### Hardness Construction

We remark that without any restrictions on the parameters of the problem,
in the trajectory testing problem defined in [Definition˜4.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#S4.Thmdefinition1 "Definition 4.1 (Trajectory Testing Problem). ‣ 4 Low-Degree Polynomial Hardness ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise"),
the TV distance between H0H\_{0} and H1H\_{1} could be arbitrarily small,
in which case the problem would be hard to solve even information-theoretically.
Here we construct D(0)D^{(0)} and 𝒟\mathcal{D} such that any algorithm that has a good error guarantee under the drift model can be used to solve the testing problem. Specifically, we consider examples supported over {±1}d\{\pm 1\}^{d}, for γ=Θ​(1/d)\gamma=\Theta(1/d) and force the marginal distribution to be the uniform distribution over the hypercube.

###### Definition 4.3(Hard Instance).

Let T∈ℕ+T\in\mathbb{N}^{+} be any sufficiently large number, let Δ∈(0,1)\Delta\in(0,1), γ<log−1⁡(1/Δ)\gamma<\log^{-1}(1/\Delta), and let d=Θ​(1/γ)d=\Theta(1/\gamma) be an odd number. We construct an instance of trajectory testing as follows.

1. 1.


Null hypothesisH0:H\_{0}: For each i∈\[T\]i\in\[T\], Dx(0)D^{(0)}\_{x} is the uniform distribution over {±1}d\{\pm 1\}^{d} and 𝐏𝐫Di(0)\[y​(x)=1\]=1−η\operatorname\*{\mathbf{Pr}}\_{D^{(0)}\_{i}}\[y(x)=1\]=1-\eta.

2. 2.


Alternative hypothesisH1:H\_{1}: Let SS be a set of 2dc2^{d^{c}} vectors in {±1}d\{\pm 1\}^{d} such that for every v,u∈Sv,u\in S and v≠uv\neq u, \|v⋅u\|≤d1/2+c\left\|v\cdot u\right\|\leq d^{1/2+c}, c∈(0,1/2)c\in(0,1/2).
For every v∈Sv\in S, we define the distribution D(v)D^{(v)} as follows. For i≤T−mi\leq T-m, (Di(v))x(D^{(v)}\_{i})\_{x} is the uniform distribution over {±1}d\{\pm 1\}^{d} and 𝐏𝐫Di(v)\[y​(x)=1\]=1−η\operatorname\*{\mathbf{Pr}}\_{D^{(v)}\_{i}}\[y(x)=1\]=1-\eta. For i=T−m+ji=T-m+j, j∈\[m\]j\in\[m\], we define the ground truth halfspace to be hi(v)=sign​(v⋅x+ti),h^{(v)}\_{i}=\mathrm{sign}(v\cdot x+t\_{i}), where 𝐏𝐫Di(v)\[hi(v)​(x)=−1\]=Δ​j\operatorname\*{\mathbf{Pr}}\_{D^{(v)}\_{i}}\[h^{(v)}\_{i}(x)=-1\]=\Delta j and 𝐏𝐫Di(v)\[hi(v)​(x)≠y​(x)∣x\]=(η−Δ​j)/(1−2​Δ​j)<η\operatorname\*{\mathbf{Pr}}\_{D^{(v)}\_{i}}\[h^{(v)}\_{i}(x)\neq y(x)\mid x\]=(\eta-\Delta j)/(1-2\Delta j)<\eta. Here, m=γ−1/6​Δ−2/3m=\gamma^{-1/6}\Delta^{-2/3}.


We remark that in the construction of the hard instance, we consider halfspaces with thresholds instead of homogeneous halfspaces. This is for the purpose of simplifying the notation. Since the threshold \|t\|=O​(d)\left\|t\right\|=O(d), we are able to add O​(d)O(d) artificial dimensions to represent (w,t)∈ℝd+1(w,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1} as a single vector w¯∈ℝ2​d\bar{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{2d} such that ‖w¯‖∞<1\left\\|\bar{w}\right\\|\_{\infty}<1. This will make the halfspaces homogeneous and keep the margin γ=Θ​(1/d)\gamma=\Theta(1/d).

In [Section˜D.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#A4.SS1 "D.1 Proof of Lemma˜D.1 ‣ Appendix D Omitted Proofs from Section˜4 ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise"), we show that
for a reasonable η\eta (e.g., η=1/3\eta=1/3) the hard instance from [Definition˜4.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#S4.Thmdefinition3 "Definition 4.3 (Hard Instance). ‣ Hardness Construction ‣ 4 Low-Degree Polynomial Hardness ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise") is a valid instance of the trajectory testing problem.
In [Section˜D.5](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#A4.SS5 "D.5 From Testing to Learning ‣ Appendix D Omitted Proofs from Section˜4 ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise"), we show that any learner
that achieves error opti+Δ1/3​γ−1/6\mathrm{opt}\_{i}+\Delta^{1/3}\gamma^{-1/6}
can be used efficiently to solve the testing problem.
Thus, a low-degree polynomial hardness of the trajectory testing problem of
[Definition˜4.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#S4.Thmdefinition3 "Definition 4.3 (Hard Instance). ‣ Hardness Construction ‣ 4 Low-Degree Polynomial Hardness ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise") can be used to give a low-degree polynomial hardness
to an algorithm with an excess error o​(Δ1/3)o(\Delta^{1/3}).
Thus, our main hardness result is as follows.

###### Theorem 4.1.

Let c∈(0,1/2)c\in(0,1/2) be a constant. For η=1/3\eta=1/3 and Δ>2−1/γc\Delta>2^{-1/\gamma^{c}}, there is no polynomial p​(z)p(z) with degree less than O​(γ−c/4)O(\gamma^{-c/4}) that is a 11-distinguisher for the trajectory testing problem defined in [Definition˜4.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#S4.Thmdefinition1 "Definition 4.1 (Trajectory Testing Problem). ‣ 4 Low-Degree Polynomial Hardness ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise"), with instance constructed by [Definition˜4.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#S4.Thmdefinition3 "Definition 4.3 (Hard Instance). ‣ Hardness Construction ‣ 4 Low-Degree Polynomial Hardness ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise").

Intuitively, the hard instance constructed in [Definition˜4.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#S4.Thmdefinition3 "Definition 4.3 (Hard Instance). ‣ Hardness Construction ‣ 4 Low-Degree Polynomial Hardness ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise") requires us to distinguish two cases with RCN under a uniform distribution over the hypercube in ℝd\mathbb{R}^{d}. In the first case, the ground truth label is always +1+1. In the second case, the ground truth label remains +1+1 until time step T−mT-m. But from time step T−m+1T-m+1, the distribution starts drifting along a random direction vv. That is to say, we fix the direction of the normal vector of the target halfspace to be vv, but gradually increase the fraction of examples with ground truth label −1-1 from 0 to Θ​(Δ​m)\Theta(\Delta m). Such a construction implies that no matter how large TT is, the only examples that play a role are the last mm ones. However, due to the presence of RCN, the variance of the distributions of the labels is too large to be distinguished by a low-degree polynomial over the last mm samples.

We make use of linear algebraic tools to formalize this intuition.
Let D(0),DD^{(0)},D be distributions over ℝd\mathbb{R}^{d}. We denote by D¯:=D​(x)/D(0)​(x):ℝd→ℝ\bar{D}:=D(x)/D^{(0)}(x):\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
the relative density function between DD and D(0)D^{(0)}. Let f,g:ℝd→ℝf,g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R} be two functions. We define the inner product between f,gf,g with respect to D(0)D^{(0)} as ⟨f,g⟩D(0)=𝐄x∼D(0)f​(x)​g​(x)\langle f,g\rangle\_{D^{(0)}}=\operatorname\*{\mathbf{E}}\_{x\sim D^{(0)}}f(x)g(x) and define ‖f‖D(0)2=⟨f,f⟩D(0)\left\\|f\right\\|^{2}\_{D^{(0)}}=\langle f,f\rangle\_{D^{(0)}}. For a function f:ℝd×m→ℝf:\mathbb{R}^{d\times m}\to\mathbb{R}, we denote by f≤kf^{\leq k} the projection of ff onto the subspace of polynomials with degree at most kk with respect to D(0)D^{(0)} and denote by f≤(ℓ,k)f^{\leq(\ell,k)} the projection of ff onto the subspace of polynomials with sample degree at most (ℓ,k)(\ell,k) with respect to the inner product ⟨⋅,⋅⟩D(0)\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\_{D^{(0)}}.

###### Fact 4.1(One-sample version of Fact 2.1 and Fact 2.2 in \[ [BBH+21](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1\#bib.bibx6 "")\]).

Let 𝒞ℓ,k\mathcal{C}\_{\ell,k} be the linear subspace of polynomials over z=(z1,…,zT)z=(z\_{1},\dots,z\_{T}) with degree at most ℓ\ell in each ziz\_{i} and with each monomial having a nonzero degree in at most kk of the ziz\_{i}s. Then

|     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  | maxp∈𝒞ℓ,k​𝐄D(0)p2​(z)≤1⁡(𝐄z∼D(0)p​(z)−𝐄D∼𝒟𝐄z∼D⁡p​(z))\displaystyle\max\_{p\in\mathcal{C}\_{\ell,k}\operatorname\*{\mathbf{E}}\_{D^{(0)}}p^{2}(z)\leq 1}\left(\operatorname\*{\mathbf{E}}\_{z\sim D^{(0)}}p(z)-\operatorname\*{\mathbf{E}}\_{D\sim\mathcal{D}}\operatorname\*{\mathbf{E}}\_{z\sim D}p(z)\right) |  |
|  | =‖𝐄D∼𝒟(D¯≤ℓ,k)−1‖D(0).\displaystyle\;=\left\\|\operatorname\*{\mathbf{E}}\_{D\sim\mathcal{D}}(\bar{D}^{\leq\ell,k})-1\right\\|\_{D^{(0)}}. |  |

Notice that if ‖𝐄D∼𝒟(D¯≤ℓ,k)−1‖D(0)≤α\left\\|\operatorname\*{\mathbf{E}}\_{D\sim\mathcal{D}}(\bar{D}^{\leq\ell,k})-1\right\\|\_{D^{(0)}}\leq\alpha, then there is no polynomial p​(z)p(z) in 𝒞ℓ,k\mathcal{C}\_{\ell,k} that can distinguish D(0)D^{(0)} from 𝒟\mathcal{D} with an advantage α\alpha. Notice that 𝒞∞,k\mathcal{C}\_{\infty,k} contains the family of polynomials of degree kk. Thus, to prove [Theorem˜4.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#S4.Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 4.1. ‣ Hardness Construction ‣ 4 Low-Degree Polynomial Hardness ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise"), our goal is to show

|     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  | ‖𝐄D∼𝒟(D¯≤∞,k)−1‖D(0)2\displaystyle\left\\|\operatorname\*{\mathbf{E}}\_{D\sim\mathcal{D}}(\bar{D}^{\leq\infty,k})-1\right\\|\_{D^{(0)}}^{2} |  |
|  | =𝐄D(u),D(v)∼𝒟\[⟨(D(u)¯≤∞,k),(D(v)¯≤∞,k)⟩D(0)\]−1≤1.\displaystyle=\operatorname\*{\mathbf{E}}\_{D^{(u)},D^{(v)}\sim\mathcal{D}}\[\langle(\bar{D^{(u)}}^{\leq\infty,k}),(\bar{D^{(v)}}^{\leq\infty,k})\rangle\_{D^{(0)}}\]-1\leq 1. |  |

To do this, we first show that for every u,v∈{±1}du,v\in\{\pm 1\}^{d}, ⟨(D(u)¯≤∞,k),(D(v)¯≤∞,k)⟩D(0)−1\langle(\bar{D^{(u)}}^{\leq\infty,k}),(\bar{D^{(v)}}^{\leq\infty,k})\rangle\_{D^{(0)}}-1 only depends on the last mm time steps in the trajectory.

###### Lemma 4.1.

Under the construction of [Definition˜4.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#S4.Thmdefinition3 "Definition 4.3 (Hard Instance). ‣ Hardness Construction ‣ 4 Low-Degree Polynomial Hardness ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise"), we have for every ℓ,k\ell,k,

|     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  | ⟨(D(u)¯≤ℓ,k),(D(v)¯≤ℓ,k)⟩D(0)−1\displaystyle\langle(\bar{D^{(u)}}^{\leq\ell,k}),(\bar{D^{(v)}}^{\leq\ell,k})\rangle\_{D^{(0)}}-1 |  |

## 5 Conclusion

This paper provides information-theoretic rates and the first polynomial-time algorithms for
learning drifting halfspaces with structured classes of noise, such as the semi-random Massart
noise and its special case of random classification noise,
including the noiseless (realizable) model. Additionally, we prove lower bounds applying to
polynomial-time algorithms within the broad class of low-degree polynomial tests, providing
strong evidence of an information-computation tradeoff for this problem.

A number of interesting questions for future work remain.
For a concrete example, while polynomial-time algorithms with nontrivially bounded error
are out of reach for general distributional families of covariates and adversarial labels,
learning halfspaces under structured covariate distributions
(e.g., logconcave distributions)
and arbitrary labels is possible with polynomial-time algorithms for the settings with no
distribution drift. Are such results possible in the drifting concept model? More broadly, it
would be interesting to explore the computational aspects of learning with drift for other
natural hypothesis classes, such as intersections of halfspaces and neural networks.

## Appendix A Useful Concentration Inequalities

Our technical sections will make use of the following concentration inequalities.

###### Fact A.1(Hoeffding’s Inequality).

Let X1,…,XnX\_{1},\dots,X\_{n} be nn independent random variables such that Xi∈\[ai,bi\]X\_{i}\in\[a\_{i},b\_{i}\]. Let Sn=∑i=1nXiS\_{n}=\sum\_{i=1}^{n}X\_{i}. Then for t>0t>0, 𝐏𝐫\[\|Sn−𝐄Sn\|>t\]≤exp⁡(−2​t2/∑i=1n(ai−bi)2)\operatorname\*{\mathbf{Pr}}\left\[\left\|S\_{n}-\operatorname\*{\mathbf{E}}S\_{n}\right\|>t\right\]\leq\exp(-2t^{2}/\sum\_{i=1}^{n}(a\_{i}-b\_{i})^{2}).

###### Fact A.2(Bernstein’s Inequality).

Let X1,…,XnX\_{1},\dots,X\_{n} be independent random variables such that 𝐄Xi=0\operatorname\*{\mathbf{E}}X\_{i}=0 and \|Xi\|≤M\left\|X\_{i}\right\|\leq M. Let σ2:=∑i=1n𝐄\[Xi2\]\sigma^{2}:=\sum\_{i=1}^{n}\operatorname\*{\mathbf{E}}\[X\_{i}^{2}\].
Then for any t>0t>0, 𝐏𝐫(∑i=1nXi≥t)≤exp⁡(−t22​σ2+23​M​t).\operatorname\*{\mathbf{Pr}}\!\left(\sum\_{i=1}^{n}X\_{i}\geq t\right)\leq\exp\!\left(-\frac{t^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}+\frac{2}{3}Mt}\right).

###### Fact A.3(Talagrand’s Concentration Inequality).

Let X1,…,XnX\_{1},\dots,X\_{n} be independent random variables taking values in a measurable space 𝒳\mathcal{X}.
Let ℱ\mathcal{F} be a countable class of measurable functions
f:𝒳→\[−b,b\]f:\mathcal{X}\to\[-b,b\] such that 𝔼​\[f​(Xi)\]=0,for all ​f∈ℱ,i∈\[n\].\mathbb{E}\[f(X\_{i})\]=0,\text{for all }f\in\mathcal{F},\ i\in\[n\].
Define Z:=supf∈ℱ∑i=1nf​(Xi),Z:=\sup\_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\sum\_{i=1}^{n}f(X\_{i}), and σ2:=supf∈ℱ∑i=1n𝔼​\[f​(Xi)2\]\sigma^{2}:=\sup\_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\sum\_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\[f(X\_{i})^{2}\], then for all t>0t>0, 𝐏𝐫(Z≥𝔼​\[Z\]+t)≤exp⁡(−t22​σ2+2​b​t).\operatorname\*{\mathbf{Pr}}\!\left(Z\geq\mathbb{E}\[Z\]+t\right)\leq\exp\!\left(-\frac{t^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}+2b\,t}\right).

###### Fact A.4(Dudley’s Inequality).

Let (ℱ,ρ)(\mathcal{F},\rho) be a metric space and let
{Xf:f∈ℱ}\{X\_{f}:f\in\mathcal{F}\} be a centered stochastic process
(i.e., 𝐄Xf=0\operatorname\*{\mathbf{E}}X\_{f}=0 for all ff).
Assume the process has sub-Gaussian increments with respect to ρ\rho, then
𝐄\[supf∈ℱXf\]≤∫0diam⁡(ℱ,ρ)log⁡𝒩​(ϵ,ℱ,ρ)​𝑑ϵ,\operatorname\*{\mathbf{E}}\!\left\[\sup\_{f\in\mathcal{F}}X\_{f}\right\]\;\leq\;\int\_{0}^{\operatorname{diam}(\mathcal{F},\rho)}\sqrt{\log\mathcal{N}(\epsilon,\mathcal{F},\rho)}\,d\epsilon,
where 𝒩​(ϵ,ℱ,ρ)\mathcal{N}(\epsilon,\mathcal{F},\rho) is the covering number.

## Appendix B Omitted Proofs from [Section˜2](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1\#S2 "2 Information-Theoretic Baselines under Massart Noise ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise")

### B.1 Proof of [Theorem˜2.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1\#S2.Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 2.1 (Information-Theoretic Upper Bound). ‣ 2 Information-Theoretic Baselines under Massart Noise ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise")

For convenience, we restate the theorem below.

###### Theorem B.1(Restatement of [Theorem˜2.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1\#S2.Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 2.1 (Information-Theoretic Upper Bound). ‣ 2 Information-Theoretic Baselines under Massart Noise ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise")).

Consider the problem of learning under drifting concepts. Let H:X→{±1}H:X\to\{\pm 1\} be a hypothesis class with VC dimension dd. There is an algorithm 𝒜\mathcal{A} such that if for every i>0i>0, D(i)D^{(i)} is realized by ((h∗)(i),ηi​(x))((h^{\*})^{(i)},\eta\_{i}(x)) for (h∗)(i)(h^{\*})^{(i)} and ηi\eta\_{i} satisfies the η\eta-Massart noise condition, then for every t>Ω​(d/((1−2​η)​Δ))t>\Omega(d/((1-2\eta)\Delta)), 𝒜\mathcal{A} outputs a hypothesis h^(t)\hat{h}^{(t)} with error at most optt+O~​((d​Δ/(1−2​η))1/2)\mathrm{opt}\_{t}+\tilde{O}((d\Delta/(1-2\eta))^{1/2}).

The algorithm we will analyze, [Algorithm˜3](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#alg3 "In B.1 Proof of Theorem˜2.1 ‣ Appendix B Omitted Proofs from Section˜2 ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise"), works in epochs, where the i+1i+1th epoch corresponds to time steps t∈{iW+1,…,(i+1)Wt\in\{iW+1,\dots,(i+1)W}. In epoch ii, [Algorithm˜3](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#alg3 "In B.1 Proof of Theorem˜2.1 ‣ Appendix B Omitted Proofs from Section˜2 ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise") maintains a hypothesis h^(i)\hat{h}^{(i)} for prediction and collects a set of examples S(i)S^{(i)} observed during this epoch. At the end of the epoch, [Algorithm˜3](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#alg3 "In B.1 Proof of Theorem˜2.1 ‣ Appendix B Omitted Proofs from Section˜2 ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise") updates the hypothesis h(i)h^{(i)} using the collected dataset S(i)S^{(i)} by running a empirical risk minimization over S(i)S^{(i)}.

Algorithm 3DriftedERM(Learning Drifting Concept with Bounded Noise)

1:Input:ℋ:\mathcal{H}: hypothesis class, Δ>0\Delta>0 drift rate, η\eta: noise rate

2:Output: For each time step t≥1t\geq 1, a hypothesis h:X→{±1}h:X\to\{\pm 1\}

3:Let W=Θ~​(d​Δ−1​(1−2​η)−1),i=1W=\tilde{\Theta}(\sqrt{d\Delta^{-1}(1-2\eta)^{-1}}),i=1, h^(0)​(x)≡1\hat{h}^{(0)}(x)\equiv 1

4:fort=1,2,…t=1,2,\dotsdo

5:  Output hypothesis h^(i−1)\hat{h}^{(i-1)} and receive (x(t),y(t))(x^{(t)},y^{(t)}).

6:S(i)←S(i)∪{(x(t),y(t))}S^{(i)}\leftarrow S^{(i)}\cup\{(x^{(t)},y^{(t)})\}

7:ift=i​Wt=iWthen

8:h^(i)←argminh∈ℋerr^​(h):=W−1​∑(x,y)∈S(i)𝟙​{h​(x)≠y}\hat{h}^{(i)}\leftarrow\operatorname\*{argmin}\_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\hat{\mathrm{err}}(h):=W^{-1}\sum\_{(x,y)\in S^{(i)}}\mathds{1}\{h(x)\neq y\}, S(i)←∅S^{(i)}\leftarrow\emptyset

9:i←i+1i\leftarrow i+1

###### Proof of [Theorem˜2.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1\#S2.Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 2.1 (Information-Theoretic Upper Bound). ‣ 2 Information-Theoretic Baselines under Massart Noise ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise").

Consider the hypotheses constructed by [Algorithm˜3](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#alg3 "In B.1 Proof of Theorem˜2.1 ‣ Appendix B Omitted Proofs from Section˜2 ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise").
The main part of the proof is to argue that for T=i​WT=iW, err(T)​(h^(i))≤optT+O~​((d​Δ/(1−2​η))1/2)\mathrm{err}^{(T)}(\hat{h}^{(i)})\leq\mathrm{opt}\_{T}+\tilde{O}((d\Delta/(1-2\eta))^{1/2}).
Suppose this is correct, then for t′=i​W+jt^{\prime}=iW+j, 1≤j≤W1\leq j\leq W, we have

|     |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  | err(t′)​(h^(i))\displaystyle\mathrm{err}^{(t^{\prime})}(\hat{h}^{(i)}) | =𝐏𝐫(x,y)∼D(t′)(h^(i)​(x)≠y)\displaystyle=\operatorname\*{\mathbf{Pr}}\_{(x,y)\sim D^{(t^{\prime})}}(\hat{h}^{(i)}(x)\neq y) |  |
|  |  | ≤𝐏𝐫(x,y)∼D(i​W)(h^(i)​(x)≠y)+j​Δ\displaystyle\leq\operatorname\*{\mathbf{Pr}}\_{(x,y)\sim D^{(iW)}}(\hat{h}^{(i)}(x)\neq y)+j\Delta |  |
|  |  | ≤opti​W+O~​((d​Δ/(1−2​η))1/2)+j​Δ\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{opt}\_{iW}+\tilde{O}((d\Delta/(1-2\eta))^{1/2})+j\Delta |  |
|  |  | ≤optt′+O~​((d​Δ/(1−2​η))1/2)+2​j​Δ\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{opt}\_{t^{\prime}}+\tilde{O}((d\Delta/(1-2\eta))^{1/2})+2j\Delta |  |
|  |  | =optt′+O~​((d​Δ/(1−2​η))1/2),\displaystyle=\mathrm{opt}\_{t^{\prime}}+\tilde{O}((d\Delta/(1-2\eta))^{1/2}), |  |

where the first inequality comes from the drifting model assumption, the second inequality comes from assuming that for T=i​WT=iW (to be argued in the rest of the proof), err(T)​(h^(i))≤optT+O~​((d​Δ/(1−2​η))1/2)\mathrm{err}^{(T)}(\hat{h}^{(i)})\leq\mathrm{opt}\_{T}+\tilde{O}((d\Delta/(1-2\eta))^{1/2}), and the third inequality again uses the bounded drift assumption.

In the rest of the proof, we argue that err(T)​(h^(i))≤optT+O~​((d​Δ/(1−2​η))1/2)\mathrm{err}^{(T)}(\hat{h}^{(i)})\leq\mathrm{opt}\_{T}+\tilde{O}((d\Delta/(1-2\eta))^{1/2}) for T=i​W.T=iW.

For i≥1i\geq 1,
let T=i​WT=iW and let h(T)h^{(T)} be the hypothesis that realizes D(T)D^{(T)}.
For each tj=(i−1)​W+jt\_{j}=(i-1)W+j and h∈ℋh\in\mathcal{H},
define diffj​(h):=𝟙​{h​(x(tj))≠y(tj)}−𝟙​{h(T)​(x(tj))≠y(tj)}\mathrm{diff}\_{j}(h):=\mathds{1}\{h(x^{(t\_{j})})\neq y^{(t\_{j})}\}-\mathds{1}\{h^{(T)}(x^{(t\_{j})})\neq y^{(t\_{j})}\}.
This gives

|     |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  | 1W​∑j=1Wdiffj​(h)=err^​(h)−err^​(h(T)),\displaystyle\frac{1}{W}\sum\_{j=1}^{W}\mathrm{diff}\_{j}(h)=\hat{\mathrm{err}}(h)-\hat{\mathrm{err}}(h^{(T)}), |  | (1) |

and thus W−1​∑j=1Wdiffj​(h^(i))≤0W^{-1}\sum\_{j=1}^{W}\mathrm{diff}\_{j}(\hat{h}^{(i)})\leq 0 (by the definition of h^(i)\hat{h}^{(i)} in [Algorithm˜3](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#alg3 "In B.1 Proof of Theorem˜2.1 ‣ Appendix B Omitted Proofs from Section˜2 ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise")).

Take expectations for each time step tt on both sides of ( [1](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#A2.E1 "Equation 1 ‣ Proof of Theorem˜2.1. ‣ B.1 Proof of Theorem˜2.1 ‣ Appendix B Omitted Proofs from Section˜2 ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise")), we get

|     |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  |  | 𝐄\[1W​∑j=1Wdiffj​(h)\]\displaystyle\operatorname\*{\mathbf{E}}\bigg\[\frac{1}{W}\sum\_{j=1}^{W}\mathrm{diff}\_{j}(h)\bigg\] |  |
|  | =\displaystyle=\; | 𝐄\[err^​(h)\]−𝐄\[err^​(h(T))\]\displaystyle\operatorname\*{\mathbf{E}}\[\hat{\mathrm{err}}(h)\]-\operatorname\*{\mathbf{E}}\[\hat{\mathrm{err}}(h^{(T)})\] |  |
|  | =\displaystyle=\; | 1W​∑t=(i−1)​W+1T𝐏𝐫(x(t),y(t))∼D(t)(h​(x(t))≠y(t))−1W​∑t=(i−1)​W+1T𝐏𝐫(x(t),y(t))∼D(t)(h(T)​(x(t))≠y(t)).\displaystyle\frac{1}{W}\sum\_{t=(i-1)W+1}^{T}\operatorname\*{\mathbf{Pr}}\_{(x^{(t)},y^{(t)})\sim D^{(t)}}(h(x^{(t)})\neq y^{(t)})-\frac{1}{W}\sum\_{t=(i-1)W+1}^{T}\operatorname\*{\mathbf{Pr}}\_{(x^{(t)},y^{(t)})\sim D^{(t)}}(h^{(T)}(x^{(t)})\neq y^{(t)}). |  |

By the drifting assumption and triangle inequality, we have dTV​(D(t),D(T))≤W​Δd\_{\mathrm{TV}}\!\big(D^{(t)},D^{(T)}\big)\leq W\Delta for any t=(i−1)​W+j,j∈\[W\]t=(i-1)W+j,\,j\in\[W\].
This implies that
for any h∈ℋh\in\mathcal{H}, we have

|     |     |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  |  | \|1W​∑t=(i−1)​W+1T𝐏𝐫(x(t),y(t))∼D(t)(h​(x(t))≠y(t))−𝐏𝐫(x(T),y(T))∼D(T)(h​(x(T))≠y(T))\|\displaystyle\bigg\|\frac{1}{W}\sum\_{t=(i-1)W+1}^{T}\operatorname\*{\mathbf{Pr}}\_{(x^{(t)},y^{(t)})\sim D^{(t)}}(h(x^{(t)})\neq y^{(t)})-\operatorname\*{\mathbf{Pr}}\_{(x^{(T)},y^{(T)})\sim D^{(T)}}(h(x^{(T)})\neq y^{(T)})\bigg\| |  |
|  | ≤\displaystyle\leq\; | 1W​∑t=(i−1)​W+1T\|𝐏𝐫(x(t),y(t))∼D(t)(h​(x(t))≠y(t))−𝐏𝐫(x(T),y(T))∼D(T)(h​(x(T))≠y(T))\|\displaystyle\frac{1}{W}\sum\_{t=(i-1)W+1}^{T}\bigg\|\operatorname\*{\mathbf{Pr}}\_{(x^{(t)},y^{(t)})\sim D^{(t)}}(h(x^{(t)})\neq y^{(t)})-\operatorname\*{\mathbf{Pr}}\_{(x^{(T)},y^{(T)})\sim D^{(T)}}(h(x^{(T)})\neq y^{(T)})\bigg\| |  |
