###### Fact D.1(Lemma 3.5 in \[ [DDK+23](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1\#bib.bibx14 "")\]).

For v,u∈{±1}dv,u\in\{\pm 1\}^{d} such that \|v⋅u\|≤d1−c\left\|v\cdot u\right\|\leq d^{1-c} and halfspace fv​(x),fu​(x)f\_{v}(x),f\_{u}(x) satisfies 𝐏𝐫x∼{±1}d(fv​(x)=1)=𝐏𝐫x∼{±1}d(fv​(x)=1)=ϵ\operatorname\*{\mathbf{Pr}}\_{x\sim\{\pm 1\}^{d}}(f\_{v}(x)=1)=\operatorname\*{\mathbf{Pr}}\_{x\sim\{\pm 1\}^{d}}(f\_{v}(x)=1)=\epsilon, ϵ∈(0,1)\epsilon\in(0,1) small enough, there is some absolute constant C>0C>0 such that 𝐄x∼{±1}d𝟙​{fv​(x)=1}​𝟙​{fu​(x)=1}≤C​log2⁡(d/ϵ)​ϵ2​\|v⋅u\|/d+ϵ2\operatorname\*{\mathbf{E}}\_{x\sim\{\pm 1\}^{d}}\mathds{1}\{f\_{v}(x)=1\}\mathds{1}\{f\_{u}(x)=1\}\leq C\log^{2}(d/\epsilon)\epsilon^{2}\left\|v\cdot u\right\|/d+\epsilon^{2}.

Applying [˜D.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#A4.Thmfact1 "Fact D.1 (Lemma 3.5 in [DDK+23]). ‣ Proof of Lemma˜4.2. ‣ D.3 Proof of Lemma˜4.2 ‣ Appendix D Omitted Proofs from Section˜4 ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise") to −hi(v),−hi(u)-h^{(v)}\_{i},-h^{(u)}\_{i} with ϵ=Δ​j\epsilon=\Delta j, we have

|     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  | 𝐄Di(0)((𝟙{hi(v)(x)=−1}(𝟙{hi(u)(x)=−1}−(Δj)2))≤O~(d−(1/2−c)(Δj)2).\displaystyle\operatorname\*{\mathbf{E}}\_{D\_{i}^{(0)}}\left((\mathds{1}\{h^{(v)}\_{i}(x)=-1\}(\mathds{1}\{h^{(u)}\_{i}(x)=-1\}-(\Delta j)^{2})\right)\leq\tilde{O}(d^{-(1/2-c)}(\Delta j)^{2})\;. |  |

∎

### D.4 Proof of [Theorem˜4.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1\#S4.Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 4.1. ‣ Hardness Construction ‣ 4 Low-Degree Polynomial Hardness ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise")

We restate the theorem below.

###### Theorem D.1(Restatement of [Theorem˜4.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1\#S4.Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 4.1. ‣ Hardness Construction ‣ 4 Low-Degree Polynomial Hardness ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise")).

Let c∈(0,1/2)c\in(0,1/2) be a constant. For η=1/3\eta=1/3 and Δ>2−1/γc\Delta>2^{-1/\gamma^{c}}, there is no polynomial p​(z)p(z) with degree less than O​(γ−c/4)O(\gamma^{-c/4}) that is a 11-distinguisher for the trajectory testing problem defined in [Definition˜4.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#S4.Thmdefinition1 "Definition 4.1 (Trajectory Testing Problem). ‣ 4 Low-Degree Polynomial Hardness ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise"), with instance constructed by [Definition˜4.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#S4.Thmdefinition3 "Definition 4.3 (Hard Instance). ‣ Hardness Construction ‣ 4 Low-Degree Polynomial Hardness ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise").

###### Proof of [Theorem˜4.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1\#S4.Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 4.1. ‣ Hardness Construction ‣ 4 Low-Degree Polynomial Hardness ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise").

By [˜4.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#S4.Thmfact1 "Fact 4.1 (One-sample version of Fact 2.1 and Fact 2.2 in [BBH+21]). ‣ Hardness Construction ‣ 4 Low-Degree Polynomial Hardness ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise"), to show there is no polynomial with degree k=O​(dc/4)k=O(d^{c/4}) that is a O​(1)O(1)-distinguisher for D(0)D^{(0)} and DD,
it suffices to upper bound ‖𝐄D∼𝒟(D¯≤∞,k)−1‖D(0)\left\\|\operatorname\*{\mathbf{E}}\_{D\sim\mathcal{D}}(\bar{D}^{\leq\infty,k})-1\right\\|\_{D^{(0)}} for D(0)D^{(0)} and 𝒟\mathcal{D} constructed in [Definition˜4.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#S4.Thmdefinition3 "Definition 4.3 (Hard Instance). ‣ Hardness Construction ‣ 4 Low-Degree Polynomial Hardness ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise"). We have

|     |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  | ‖𝐄D∼𝒟(D¯≤∞,k)−1‖D(0)2\displaystyle\left\\|\operatorname\*{\mathbf{E}}\_{D\sim\mathcal{D}}(\bar{D}^{\leq\infty,k})-1\right\\|\_{D^{(0)}}^{2} | =⟨𝐄D(u)∼𝒟(D(u)¯≤∞,k)−1,𝐄D(v)∼𝒟(D(v)¯≤∞,k)−1⟩D(0)\displaystyle=\langle\operatorname\*{\mathbf{E}}\_{D^{(u)}\sim\mathcal{D}}(\bar{D^{(u)}}^{\leq\infty,k})-1,\operatorname\*{\mathbf{E}}\_{D^{(v)}\sim\mathcal{D}}(\bar{D^{(v)}}^{\leq\infty,k})-1\rangle\_{D^{(0)}} |  |
|  |  | =⟨𝐄D(u)∼𝒟(D(u)¯≤∞,k),𝐄D(v)∼𝒟(D(v)¯≤∞,k)⟩D(0)−1\displaystyle=\langle\operatorname\*{\mathbf{E}}\_{D^{(u)}\sim\mathcal{D}}(\bar{D^{(u)}}^{\leq\infty,k}),\operatorname\*{\mathbf{E}}\_{D^{(v)}\sim\mathcal{D}}(\bar{D^{(v)}}^{\leq\infty,k})\rangle\_{D^{(0)}}-1 |  |
|  |  | =𝐄D(u),D(v)∼𝒟⟨(D(u)¯≤∞,k),(D(v)¯≤∞,k)⟩D(0)−1,\displaystyle=\operatorname\*{\mathbf{E}}\_{D^{(u)},D^{(v)}\sim\mathcal{D}}\langle(\bar{D^{(u)}}^{\leq\infty,k}),(\bar{D^{(v)}}^{\leq\infty,k})\rangle\_{D^{(0)}}-1, |  |

where in the second equality, we use the fact that ⟨D(u)¯≤∞,k,1⟩D(0)=1\langle\bar{D^{(u)}}^{\leq\infty,k},1\rangle\_{D^{(0)}}=1. By [Lemma˜4.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#S4.Thmlemma1 "Lemma 4.1. ‣ Hardness Construction ‣ 4 Low-Degree Polynomial Hardness ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise"), we have

|     |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  |  | 𝐄D(u),D(v)∼𝒟⟨(D(u)¯≤∞,k),(D(v)¯≤∞,k)⟩D(0)−1\displaystyle\operatorname\*{\mathbf{E}}\_{D^{(u)},D^{(v)}\sim\mathcal{D}}\langle(\bar{D^{(u)}}^{\leq\infty,k}),(\bar{D^{(v)}}^{\leq\infty,k})\rangle\_{D^{(0)}}-1 |  |
|  | =\displaystyle= | ∑A⊆\[m\],A≠∅,\|A\|≤k𝐄D(u),D(v)∼𝒟∏j∈\[A\](⟨DT−m+j(u)¯(zi)),DT−m+j(v)¯(zi)⟩Di(0)−1).\displaystyle\sum\_{A\subseteq\[m\],A\neq\emptyset,\|A\|\leq k}\operatorname\*{\mathbf{E}}\_{D^{(u)},D^{(v)}\sim\mathcal{D}}\prod\_{j\in\[A\]}\left(\langle\bar{D^{(u)}\_{T-m+j}}(z\_{i})),\bar{D^{(v)}\_{T-m+j}}(z\_{i})\rangle\_{D\_{i}^{(0)}}-1\right). |  |

Thus, to conclude the proof, it remain to upper bound

|     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  | 𝐄D(u),D(v)∼𝒟∏j∈\[A\](⟨DT−m+j(u)¯(zi)),DT−m+j(v)¯(zi)⟩Di(0)−1).\displaystyle\operatorname\*{\mathbf{E}}\_{D^{(u)},D^{(v)}\sim\mathcal{D}}\prod\_{j\in\[A\]}\left(\langle\bar{D^{(u)}\_{T-m+j}}(z\_{i})),\bar{D^{(v)}\_{T-m+j}}(z\_{i})\rangle\_{D\_{i}^{(0)}}-1\right). |  |

We have

|     |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  |  | 𝐄D(u),D(v)∼𝒟∏j∈\[A\](⟨DT−m+j(u)¯(zi)),DT−m+j(v)¯(zi)⟩Di(0)−1)\displaystyle\operatorname\*{\mathbf{E}}\_{D^{(u)},D^{(v)}\sim\mathcal{D}}\prod\_{j\in\[A\]}\left(\langle\bar{D^{(u)}\_{T-m+j}}(z\_{i})),\bar{D^{(v)}\_{T-m+j}}(z\_{i})\rangle\_{D\_{i}^{(0)}}-1\right) |  |
|  | =\displaystyle= | 1\|S\|​∏j∈\[A\](Δ​j)+(1−1\|S\|)​∏j∈\[A\](d−(1/2−c)​(Δ​j)2)\displaystyle\frac{1}{\|S\|}\prod\_{j\in\[A\]}(\Delta j)+(1-\frac{1}{\|S\|})\prod\_{j\in\[A\]}(d^{-(1/2-c)}(\Delta j)^{2}) |  |
|  | ≤\displaystyle\leq | 2−Ω​(dc)​(Δ​m)\|A\|+(d−(1/2−c)​(Δ​m)2)\|A\|\displaystyle 2^{-\Omega(d^{c})}(\Delta m)^{\|A\|}+(d^{-(1/2-c)}(\Delta m)^{2})^{\|A\|} |  |
|  | ≤\displaystyle\leq | 2−Ω​(dc)​(d1/6​Δ1/3)\|A\|+(d−(1/2−c)​\|A\|)​(d1/6​Δ1/3)2​\|A\|\displaystyle 2^{-\Omega(d^{c})}(d^{1/6}\Delta^{1/3})^{\|A\|}+(d^{-(1/2-c)\|A\|})(d^{1/6}\Delta^{1/3})^{2\|A\|} |  |

Here, the inequality follows the choice of m=d1/6​Δ−2/3m=d^{1/6}\Delta^{-2/3}. When \|A\|≤O​(dc/4)\|A\|\leq O(d^{c/4}), we have

|     |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  |  | 2−Ω​(dc)​(d1/6​Δ1/3)\|A\|+(d−(1/2−c)​\|A\|)​(d1/6​Δ1/3)2​\|A\|\displaystyle 2^{-\Omega(d^{c})}(d^{1/6}\Delta^{1/3})^{\|A\|}+(d^{-(1/2-c)\|A\|})(d^{1/6}\Delta^{1/3})^{2\|A\|} |  |
|  | =\displaystyle= | 2−Ω​(dc)​(d1/6​Δ1/3)\|A\|+(d−1/6+c​Δ2/3)\|A\|≤(d−1/6​Δ2/3)\|A\|=(1/100​m)\|A\|.\displaystyle 2^{-\Omega(d^{c})}(d^{1/6}\Delta^{1/3})^{\|A\|}+(d^{-1/6+c}\Delta^{2/3})^{\|A\|}\leq(d^{-1/6}\Delta^{2/3})^{\|A\|}=(1/100m)^{\|A\|}. |  |

This implies that by the choice of k=dc/4k=d^{c/4},

|     |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  |  | 𝐄D(u),D(v)∼𝒟⟨(D(u)¯≤∞,k),(D(v)¯≤∞,k)⟩D(0)−1\displaystyle\operatorname\*{\mathbf{E}}\_{D^{(u)},D^{(v)}\sim\mathcal{D}}\langle(\bar{D^{(u)}}^{\leq\infty,k}),(\bar{D^{(v)}}^{\leq\infty,k})\rangle\_{D^{(0)}}-1 |  |
|  | =\displaystyle= | ∑A⊆\[m\],A≠∅,\|A\|≤k𝐄D(u),D(v)∼𝒟∏j∈\[A\](⟨DT−m+j(u)¯(zi)),DT−m+j(v)¯(zi)⟩Di(0)−1)≤∑ℓ=1k(mℓ)(1/(100m))ℓ≤1.\displaystyle\sum\_{A\subseteq\[m\],A\neq\emptyset,\|A\|\leq k}\operatorname\*{\mathbf{E}}\_{D^{(u)},D^{(v)}\sim\mathcal{D}}\prod\_{j\in\[A\]}\left(\langle\bar{D^{(u)}\_{T-m+j}}(z\_{i})),\bar{D^{(v)}\_{T-m+j}}(z\_{i})\rangle\_{D\_{i}^{(0)}}-1\right)\leq\sum\_{\ell=1}^{k}\binom{m}{\ell}(1/(100m))^{\ell}\leq 1. |  |

∎

### D.5 From Testing to Learning

###### Lemma D.4.

Let 𝒜\mathcal{A} be any algorithm such that for any instance of learning γ\gamma-margin halfspaces under η\eta-RCN it achieves error opti+Δ1/3​γ−1/6\mathrm{opt}\_{i}+\Delta^{1/3}\gamma^{-1/6} for i≥Ti\geq T. Then 𝒜\mathcal{A} can be used to efficiently solve the instance of trajectory testing problem defined in [Definition˜4.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#S4.Thmdefinition3 "Definition 4.3 (Hard Instance). ‣ Hardness Construction ‣ 4 Low-Degree Polynomial Hardness ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise").

###### Proof of [Lemma˜D.4](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1\#A4.Thmlemma4 "Lemma D.4. ‣ D.5 From Testing to Learning ‣ Appendix D Omitted Proofs from Section˜4 ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise").

We notice that by the choice of m=d1/6​Δ−2/3m=d^{1/6}\Delta^{-2/3} and the design of Di(v)D^{(v)}\_{i}, we have 𝐏𝐫DT(v)(hT(v)​(x)=−1)=Δ​m=d1/6​Δ1/3\operatorname\*{\mathbf{Pr}}\_{D^{(v)}\_{T}}(h^{(v)}\_{T}(x)=-1)=\Delta m=d^{1/6}\Delta^{1/3}. Now, let h^T\hat{h}\_{T} be the hypothesis output by 𝒜\mathcal{A} in the TT-th round under H1H\_{1}. We have

|     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  | errT​(h^T)=optT+(1−2​o​p​tT)​𝐏𝐫DT(v)(hT(v)​(x)≠h^T)≤optT+d1/6​Δ1/3/100.\displaystyle\mathrm{err}\_{T}(\hat{h}\_{T})=\mathrm{opt}\_{T}+(1-2\mathrm{opt}\_{T})\operatorname\*{\mathbf{Pr}}\_{D^{(v)}\_{T}}(h^{(v)}\_{T}(x)\neq\hat{h}\_{T})\leq\mathrm{opt}\_{T}+d^{1/6}\Delta^{1/3}/100. |  |

This implies 𝐏𝐫DT(v)(hT(v)​(x)≠h^T)≤d1/6​Δ1/3/20\operatorname\*{\mathbf{Pr}}\_{D^{(v)}\_{T}}(h^{(v)}\_{T}(x)\neq\hat{h}\_{T})\leq d^{1/6}\Delta^{1/3}/20 and

|     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  | 𝐏𝐫DT(v)(h^T≠1)≥𝐏𝐫DT(v)(hT(v)​(x)≠1)−𝐏𝐫DT(v)(hT(v)​(x)≠h^T)≥19​d1/6​Δ1/3/20.\displaystyle\operatorname\*{\mathbf{Pr}}\_{D^{(v)}\_{T}}(\hat{h}\_{T}\neq 1)\geq\operatorname\*{\mathbf{Pr}}\_{D^{(v)}\_{T}}(h^{(v)}\_{T}(x)\neq 1)-\operatorname\*{\mathbf{Pr}}\_{D^{(v)}\_{T}}(h^{(v)}\_{T}(x)\neq\hat{h}\_{T})\geq 19d^{1/6}\Delta^{1/3}/20. |  |

Similarly, under H0H\_{0}, we have

|     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  | errT​(h^T)=η+(1−2​η)​𝐏𝐫DT(0)(h^T≠1)≤η+d1/6​Δ1/3/100,\displaystyle\mathrm{err}\_{T}(\hat{h}\_{T})=\eta+(1-2\eta)\operatorname\*{\mathbf{Pr}}\_{D^{(0)}\_{T}}(\hat{h}\_{T}\neq 1)\leq\eta+d^{1/6}\Delta^{1/3}/100, |  |

which implies that 𝐏𝐫DT(v)(h^T≠1)≤3​d1/6​Δ1/3/100\operatorname\*{\mathbf{Pr}}\_{D^{(v)}\_{T}}(\hat{h}\_{T}\neq 1)\leq 3d^{1/6}\Delta^{1/3}/100. This implies by running 𝒜\mathcal{A} over z=(z1,z2,…,zT)∼Dz=(z\_{1},z\_{2},\dots,z\_{T})\sim D and checking the probability of 𝐏𝐫DT(v)(h^T≠1)\operatorname\*{\mathbf{Pr}}\_{D^{(v)}\_{T}}(\hat{h}\_{T}\neq 1), we are able to distinguish H0H\_{0} and H1H\_{1}.

∎

To conclude [Theorem˜1.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#S1.Thmtheorem2 "Theorem 1.2 (Informal Implication of Theorem˜4.1). ‣ 1.1 Our Results ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise"), we make use of the
low-degree polynomial hardness conjecture. Roughly speaking, the low-degree polynomial hardness conjecture says that for a natural high-dimensional statistical testing problem of size nn, if there is no polynomial of degree O​(log⁡n)O(\log n) that can solve the testing problem, then there is no polynomial-time algorithm that can solve it either.
Originally stated in \[ [Hop18](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx29 "")\], variants of the conjecture have been used to give evidence of hardness for many problems, such as \[ [DKWB21](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx22 ""), [AV23](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx4 ""), [DKWB24](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#bib.bibx23 "")\]. We summarize the conjecture informally below.

###### Conjecture D.1.

Let 𝒫n\mathcal{P}\_{n} and 𝒬n\mathcal{Q}\_{n} be two sequences of natural distributions of size nn, if there is some ϵ>0\epsilon>0 such that there is no (log⁡n)1+ϵ(\log n)^{1+\epsilon}-degree polynomial as n→∞n\to\infty, which can distinguish 𝒫n\mathcal{P}\_{n} and 𝒬n\mathcal{Q}\_{n}, then there is no polynomial time algorithm that can distinguish 𝒫n\mathcal{P}\_{n} and 𝒬n\mathcal{Q}\_{n} with probability 1−o​(1)1-o(1).

By [Theorem˜4.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#S4.Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 4.1. ‣ Hardness Construction ‣ 4 Low-Degree Polynomial Hardness ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise"), we know that there is no polynomial of degree less than O​(γ−1/8)O(\gamma^{-1/8}) that can solve the trajectory testing problem and thus there is no polynomial time algorithm that can distinguish the trajectory testing problem. Thus, [Lemma˜D.4](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.11149v1#A4.Thmlemma4 "Lemma D.4. ‣ D.5 From Testing to Learning ‣ Appendix D Omitted Proofs from Section˜4 ‣ Efficiently Learning Drifting Halfspaces with Massart Noise") implies that there is no polynomial-time algorithm that can solve the learning problem with error optT+Δ1/3​γ−1/6\mathrm{opt}\_{T}+\Delta^{1/3}\gamma^{-1/6}.
